      - **[L4] (p.1) Introduction**
              - [L8] (p.1) Software engineering tasks are rarely unconstrained objectives.
              - [L8] (p.1) Developers ask agents not only to fix the bug, but also
            - [L7] (p.1) to preserve APIs, reproduce before patching, run specific tests, avoid prohibited tools, and report what was actually
                - [L9] (p.1) executed.
                - [L9] (p.1) For coding agents, success is therefore not merely to solve the task, but to solve it under explicit engineering
              - [L8] (p.1) constraints.

            - [L7] (p.3) from an a priori schema.
            - [L7] (p.3) We first collected a corpus of agentic coding interactions—task instructions, inline review
              - [L8] (p.3) comments, and post-hoc feedback on agent trajectories—and performed grounded open-label annotation to extract the
                - [L9] (p.3) engineering constraints that developers impose on coding agents in practice.

              - [L8] (p.3) iteratively refined this raw label space by clustering near-duplicate labels, resolving boundary cases, and stress-testing

                - [L9] (p.4) Throughout this refinement, the taxonomy is treated as an evaluation substrate rather than a descriptive catalog, guided by
                - [L9] (p.4) five design principles.
                - [L9] (p.4) Coverage
                - [L9] (p.4) :
                - [L9] (p.4) the taxonomy should span the major constraint types observed in real developer data,
                - [L9] (p.4) so that practical instructions map into it with minimal residue.
                - [L9] (p.4) Semantic exclusivity
                - [L9] (p.4) :
                - [L9] (p.4) leaf categories should be as disjoint
                - [L9] (p.4) as possible, reducing ambiguity during annotation and automatic mapping.
                - [L9] (p.4) Operationalisability
                - [L9] (p.4) :
                - [L9] (p.4) each leaf should admit
                - [L9] (p.4) concrete, testable instantiations with stable pass conditions under either rule-based or LLM-based judging.
                - [L9] (p.4) Hierarchical
                - [L9] (p.4) organisation
                - [L9] (p.4) :
                - [L9] (p.4) the structure should support both fine-grained diagnosis at the leaf level and coarse-grained reporting at the
              - [L8] (p.4) category level.
              - [L8] (p.4) Language neutrality
              - [L8] (p.4) :
              - [L8] (p.4) the same conceptual categories should transfer across the multilingual repository
              - [L8] (p.4) settings covered by ANCHOR.
                - [L9] (p.4) At the top level, the ten categories capture complementary dimensions of agentic coding.
                - [L9] (p.4) C1 (Tool Execution Protocol)
                - [L9] (p.4) governs how tools are invoked, including tool selection, invocation parameters, failure handling, and result verification,

                - [L9] (p.7) Step 4:
                - [L9] (p.7) Composite construction.
                - [L9] (p.7) Real developer instructions are often not flat lists of independent requirements; they
                - [L9] (p.7) specify joint satisfaction, required execution order, or precedence among competing requirements.
                - [L9] (p.7) ANCHOR captures
              - [L8] (p.7) this structure by composing task-grounded atomics into connected composite graphs whose edges carry one of three
                - [L9] (p.7) typed relations:

              - [L8] (p.7) THEN

              - [L8] (p.7) PRIORITY

              - [L8] (p.7) PRIORITY

              - [L8] (p.7) priority target is sacrificed to it.

            - [L7] (p.7) This

                - [L9] (p.7) We sampled 50 benchmark instances.
                - [L9] (p.7) All five engineers independently labelled every atomic constraint in

              - [L8] (p.7) They were the same senior engineers

            - [L7] (p.7) The rubric was as follows.

        - **[L5] (p.12) Main Results:**
        - **[L5] (p.12) The Constraint–Completion Gap**
                - [L9] (p.12) Table
                - [L9] (p.12) 5
                - [L9] (p.12) summarizes task completion and constraint compliance for all evaluated agent configurations.
                - [L9] (p.12) The main result is
              - [L8] (p.12) a consistent gap between producing a functionally correct patch and following the engineering constraints that govern
              - [L8] (p.12) how the patch should be produced.
              - [L8] (p.12) Claude Opus 4.8 under Cline achieves the highest reported compliance scores:
              - [L8] (p.12) 70.1% ACR, 63.7% CCR, 69.0% IPR,
            - [L7] (p.12) and 19.0% FCCR, with 43.6% constrained task completion.
            - [L7] (p.12) Its literal all-item FCCR is 7.0 percentage points higher
              - [L8] (p.12) than GPT-5.4 under Cline (12.0%).
              - [L8] (p.12) Nevertheless, 81.0% of tasks still fail at least one checklist item.
              - [L8] (p.12) Completion and
                - [L9] (p.12) FCCR are different marginal rates, not a measured joint success rate; their 24.6-point difference lower-bounds the share
                - [L9] (p.12) of tasks that are completed without passing the entire checklist.
                - [L9] (p.12) The strict interpretation of FCCR, including overridden
              - [L8] (p.12) lower-priority atomics, is specified in Section
              - [L8] (p.12) 5.2
              - [L8] (p.12) .

      - **[L4] (p.19) 7**
      - **[L4] (p.19) Discussion and Limitations**
        - **[L5] (p.19) 7.1**
        - **[L5] (p.19) Why Task Completion Is Not Deployment Readiness**
              - [L8] (p.19) Task completion is a necessary signal of coding ability, but it is not yet a sufficient signal of deployment readiness.
              - [L8] (p.19) In
                - [L9] (p.19) real software engineering, the acceptability of a patch depends not only on whether it resolves the underlying issue, but
                - [L9] (p.19) also on whether it respects the engineering constraints under which the task was assigned.
                - [L9] (p.19) Consider modification scope:
              - [L8] (p.19) an agent may fix a bug in
              - [L8] (p.19) parser.py
              - [L8] (p.19) , yet also refactor adjacent modules despite an instruction to keep changes local.
                - [L9] (p.19) The issue is resolved, but the patch becomes harder to review, riskier to backport, and less likely to be merged.
                - [L9] (p.19) Likewise,
            - [L7] (p.19) an agent may produce a functionally correct patch while violating interface constraints, for example by changing a
                - [L9] (p.19) public API when explicitly asked to preserve backward compatibility.
                - [L9] (p.19) In both cases, the artifact may satisfy a functional
              - [L8] (p.19) oracle while failing the practical acceptance criteria imposed by the developer.
                - [L9] (p.19) Process constraints create a second class of non-deployment-ready success.
                - [L9] (p.19) An agent may arrive at a correct fix without
                - [L9] (p.19) first reproducing the failure, without adding a regression test, or without reporting the commands it ran.
                - [L9] (p.19) Such a patch is

                - [L9] (p.20) Similarly, a task may be solved through prohibited means, such

              - [L8] (p.20) reproduce before fix

              - [L8] (p.20) ANCHOR is designed to make these otherwise hidden engineering failure modes
              - [L8] (p.20) measurable.

              - [L8] (p.20) therefore come apart in both directions:
              - [L8] (p.20) agents can fix the issue while behaving like weak collaborators, and they can
              - [L8] (p.20) behave in a largely disciplined way without actually completing the repair.

      - **[L4] (p.24) A**
      - **[L4] (p.24) Full Constraint Taxonomy**
                - [L9] (p.24) This appendix documents all 78 leaf categories in the current taxonomy.
                - [L9] (p.24) Across the current release, these leaves account
                - [L9] (p.24) for 2,836 instantiated atomic constraints.
                - [L9] (p.24) For each leaf, we provide:
                - [L9] (p.24) (i) a one-sentence definition of what the constraint
            - [L7] (p.24) governs, (ii) 2–4 representative instantiations drawn from real developer instructions, and (iii) the default evaluation
                - [L9] (p.24) mode (rule-based vs. LLM-as-judge).
                - [L9] (p.24) Table
                - [L9] (p.24) 2
                - [L9] (p.24) summarizes the top-level taxonomy, while Table
                - [L9] (p.24) 9
                - [L9] (p.24) provides the full mid-
              - [L8] (p.24) and leaf-level breakdown used in the current release.

        - **[L5] (p.27) B.1**
        - **[L5] (p.27) Taxonomy Design Principles**
                - [L9] (p.27) The ANCHOR taxonomy was not designed purely from an a priori schema.
                - [L9] (p.27) Instead, we started from grounded open-label
            - [L7] (p.27) annotation over real developer interactions with coding agents, including task instructions, inline review comments,
              - [L8] (p.27) and post-hoc feedback on agent trajectories.

        - **[L5] (p.29) B.4**
        - **[L5] (p.29) Composite Constraints and Query Synthesis**
            - [L7] (p.29) Real developer instructions are often not flat lists of independent requirements.
            - [L7] (p.29) They may specify joint satisfaction,
                - [L9] (p.29) required execution order, or precedence among competing requirements.
                - [L9] (p.29) ANCHOR captures this structure by organising
              - [L8] (p.29) selected subsets of atomic constraints into connected composite graphs.
              - [L8] (p.29) Edges carry one of three typed relations:
              - [L8] (p.29) AND
                - [L9] (p.29) requires joint satisfaction,
              - [L8] (p.29) THEN
                - [L9] (p.29) requires both satisfaction and correct temporal order, and
              - [L8] (p.29) PRIORITY
                - [L9] (p.29) requires the source
              - [L8] (p.29) constraint to take precedence over the target when the two are in tension.
                - [L9] (p.29) Composite constraints are introduced only when the underlying relations are semantically natural and task-meaningful;
            - [L7] (p.29) ANCHOR does not force unrelated constraints into a shared graph merely to increase structural complexity.
            - [L7] (p.29) Not all
              - [L8] (p.29) atomic constraints must participate in composites, and standalone constraints remain as independent instruction items.
            - [L7] (p.29) Edge types may be mixed within one composite,
            - [L7] (p.29) and each atomic constraint participates in at most one composite,
            - [L7] (p.29) so composites partition the related atomics into disjoint,
            - [L7] (p.29) self-contained instruction units.
            - [L7] (p.29) This connected-subgraph
              - [L8] (p.29) formulation keeps each composite interpretable as a single instruction unit rather than an arbitrary bundle of checklist
              - [L8] (p.29) clauses.
              - [L8] (p.29) The final query-synthesis stage converts the structured representation into a natural developer instruction suitable for
                - [L9] (p.29) direct agent execution.
                - [L9] (p.29) Each query is written in two parts:
                - [L9] (p.29) a short task description rewritten in the voice of a developer
              - [L8] (p.29) assigning work, and a constraint list composed of separable instruction items.
              - [L8] (p.29) Independent atomic constraints become
                - [L9] (p.29) individual items, while each composite constraint is rendered as a single natural-language item that preserves the relation

## 📖 全文内容

### 【第 1 页】


###### ANCHOR: Assessing Naturalistic Constraint Hierarchies and


###### Ordering in Repository-Grounded Agentic Coding


###### Wenqiang Zhu1,∗


###### Xinping Lei2,∗


###### Zhiyi Lai3


###### Zhengshuo Gong2


###### Tianzhuang He2


###### Songwei Yu4


###### Weihao Li1


###### Jingxuan Xu4


###### Zihang Xu5


###### Ziqi Miao3


###### Gang Wu3


###### Zhongqiu Wang3


###### Yuhui Bai3


###### Jie Li3


###### Zhao Liu3


###### Junhao Fan6


###### Yan Wu3


###### Yanan Wu2


###### Jiaheng Liu2


###### Quan Gu3


###### Ariel Deng3,†


###### 1Southern University of Science and Technology


###### 2Nanjing University


###### 3Independent Researcher


###### 4National University of Singapore


###### 5Beihang University


###### 6University of Michigan


###### Abstract


###### For repository-grounded coding agents, a functionally correct patch is not necessarily an acceptable one:


###### developers also constrain how the task must be carried out—modification scope, workflow order, validation,


###### tool use, and reporting. This capability, which we call engineering constraint following, remains largely


###### invisible to current evaluations. We introduce ANCHOR (Assessing Naturalistic Constraint Hierarchies


###### and Ordering in Repository-Grounded Agentic Coding), a benchmark built on naturalistic, multilingual


###### repository tasks spanning eight language groups. ANCHOR combines (i) a hierarchical taxonomy of


###### 10 categories and 78 leaf types distilled from real developer interactions, (ii) an automated pipeline that


###### instantiates task-grounded atomic constraints and composes them into typed AND/THEN/PRIORITY relations,


###### and (iii) a trajectory-grounded evaluation framework that checks both the code diff and the execution


###### trajectory with rule-based and LLM judges. Across 19 agent configurations spanning 2 scaffolds and 10


###### foundation models on 358 tasks, the strongest measured system, Claude Opus 4.8 under Cline, reaches


###### 70.1% atomic compliance and 63.7% relation compliance, while 19.0% of tasks pass every scored checklist


###### item under the strict all-item FCCR criterion. A five-engineer audit of constraints from 50 instances


###### reports 94% feasibility, 94% repository consistency, and 96% realism. In the primary paired set (14


###### configurations), the mean signed change in task completion after adding constraints is +0.7 percentage


###### points; individual changes vary in both directions. These results distinguish functional success from


###### constraint compliance, with failures concentrated in workflow ordering, tool-use discipline, and reporting,


###### and substantial interactions between models and scaffolds.


###### Dataset: https://huggingface.co/datasets/Yone-01/ANCHOR


###### Code: https://github.com/bupterlxp/ANCHOR


###### 1


###### Introduction


###### Software engineering tasks are rarely unconstrained objectives. Developers ask agents not only to fix the bug, but also


###### to preserve APIs, reproduce before patching, run specific tests, avoid prohibited tools, and report what was actually


###### executed. For coding agents, success is therefore not merely to solve the task, but to solve it under explicit engineering


###### constraints.


###### We argue that this is not simply generic instruction following with code context. In repository-grounded agentic


###### coding, many requirements are trajectory-grounded: they constrain tool use, workflow order, validation, and reporting


###### *Equal contribution.

###### Figure 1 Illustrative schematic. A correct patch is not necessarily an acceptable engineering solution. Two agents fix the


###### same bug and pass tests. Their observed behavior differs on API preservation, reproduction order, regression testing, and command


###### reporting. ANCHOR distinguishes task completion from engineering constraint following.


###### over the course of execution rather than only the final artifact. They are also often structured, because multiple


###### requirements must be satisfied jointly, in order, or under explicit priority. Engineering constraint following is thus a


###### distinct capability dimension, complementary to both task completion and scaffold-level policy compliance. Figure 1


###### illustrates this distinction: two trajectories may solve the same issue, yet only one is acceptable under the stated


###### engineering requirements.


###### This gap has become important because modern coding agents can already solve a substantial fraction of real repository


###### issues on SWE-bench-style benchmarks (Jimenez et al., 2024; Zan et al., 2025; Xu et al., 2025; Badertdinov et al., 2026).


###### Yet these benchmarks largely evaluate whether the final artifact is correct, not whether the agent obeyed the task-level


###### requirements that governed its execution. General instruction-following benchmarks do not capture repository-grounded,


###### multi-step engineering workflows, while recent agentic instruction-following benchmarks still emphasize generic agentic


###### instructions or scaffold-level policies rather than task-level engineering requirements (Zhou et al., 2023; Jiang et al.,


###### 2024; Pyatkin et al., 2025; Wu et al., 2025; Zou et al., 2025; Zhang et al., 2025d; Qi et al., 2025; Yao et al., 2024; Ding


###### et al., 2026). Current evaluations may therefore overestimate real-world readiness. Table 1 situates ANCHOR within


###### this landscape.


###### To address this gap, we introduce ANCHOR (Assessing Naturalistic Constraint Hierarchies and Ordering in Repository-


###### Grounded Agentic Coding), a benchmark for evaluating engineering constraint following in repository-grounded coding


###### tasks. ANCHOR combines a 10-category, 78-leaf taxonomy, an automated pipeline that instantiates atomic constraints


###### and composes them into structured AND/THEN/PRIORITY relations, and a trajectory-grounded evaluation framework


###### with rule-based and LLM judges. Across 19 constrained configurations, including a full-benchmark Claude Opus


###### 4.8 evaluation under Cline, the highest strict all-item FCCR is 19.0%. The primary paired set (14 configurations)


###### shows a mean signed completion change of +0.7 percentage points after adding constraints, with gains and losses


###### across systems. This calibration does not establish joint satisfiability or equal task difficulty; the direct evidence is


###### that functional completion and checklist compliance differ within each evaluated configuration. Our contributions are


###### to define engineering constraint following, operationalize it in a benchmark, and characterize the resulting failures,


###### supported by human audits of both generated constraints and judge decisions.

###### 2


###### Related Work


###### Repository-grounded coding benchmarks such as SWE-bench, SWE-Compass, and SWE-Rebench V2 measure whether


###### an agent can resolve real software engineering tasks in real repositories, typically through final-patch correctness under


###### an executable test harness (Jimenez et al., 2024; Xu et al., 2025; Badertdinov et al., 2026; Zan et al., 2025; Deng


###### et al., 2025; Thai et al., 2026; Yu et al., 2026; Zhou et al., 2026; Li et al., 2024). These benchmarks have driven rapid


###### progress in coding agents, but they primarily measure task completion. They therefore provide limited visibility into


###### whether an agent respected task-specific engineering requirements on scope, workflow, validation, or reporting during


###### execution. Complementary evaluations assess repository-level completion (Yang et al., 2024; Liu et al., 2025b), visual


###### and interactive artifact quality (Lei et al., 2026; Zhang et al., 2025b), and code critique (Zhang et al., 2025a). Trajectory


###### diagnosis and process feedback further expose intermediate agent failures (Li et al., 2026; Xu et al., 2026).


###### Constraint-aware instruction-following benchmarks establish the importance of explicitly verifiable requirements, both


###### in natural language and in code generation (Zhou et al., 2023; Jiang et al., 2024; Pyatkin et al., 2025; Wu et al., 2025;


###### Wen et al., 2024; Chen et al., 2024; Qin et al., 2024; Zhang et al., 2025e; Zhuo et al., 2025; Yan et al., 2025; Gunnu


###### et al., 2025). However, they mostly evaluate single-turn settings, final artifacts, or flat constraint sets. Recent work such


###### as Inverse IFEval further shows that even strong LLMs can fail to override entrenched training conventions in order to


###### follow explicit user instructions (Zhang et al., 2025c). ANCHOR instead targets repository-grounded agentic coding,


###### where many requirements are trajectory-level and many must be interpreted jointly through ordering or priority relations.


###### The closest concurrent work is OctoBench (Ding et al., 2026), which studies scaffold-aware instruction following


###### in repository-grounded coding. We view the two benchmarks as complementary rather than competing. OctoBench


###### targets scaffold- and platform-level policies, whereas ANCHOR targets task-level engineering constraints specified


###### by the developer instruction itself. ANCHOR further contributes an explicit engineering constraint taxonomy and


###### structured composite relations, enabling direct evaluation of hierarchy, ordering, and precedence. Table 1 summarises


###### this positioning against prior instruction-following and coding benchmarks.


###### 3


###### Constraint Taxonomy


###### The ANCHOR taxonomy is constructed bottom-up from real developer interactions with coding agents rather than


###### from an a priori schema. We first collected a corpus of agentic coding interactions—task instructions, inline review


###### comments, and post-hoc feedback on agent trajectories—and performed grounded open-label annotation to extract the


###### engineering constraints that developers impose on coding agents in practice. A panel of senior software engineers then


###### iteratively refined this raw label space by clustering near-duplicate labels, resolving boundary cases, and stress-testing

###### the emerging hierarchy against held-out trajectories. The resulting hierarchy contains 10 top-level categories, 34


###### mid-level subcategories, and 78 leaf categories; in the current release, these leaves account for 2,836 instantiated


###### atomic constraints. Full annotation guidelines, boundary cases, and the complete taxonomy are provided in Appendix A


###### and Appendix B.


###### Throughout this refinement, the taxonomy is treated as an evaluation substrate rather than a descriptive catalog, guided by


###### five design principles. Coverage: the taxonomy should span the major constraint types observed in real developer data,


###### so that practical instructions map into it with minimal residue. Semantic exclusivity: leaf categories should be as disjoint


###### as possible, reducing ambiguity during annotation and automatic mapping. Operationalisability: each leaf should admit


###### concrete, testable instantiations with stable pass conditions under either rule-based or LLM-based judging. Hierarchical


###### organisation: the structure should support both fine-grained diagnosis at the leaf level and coarse-grained reporting at the


###### category level. Language neutrality: the same conceptual categories should transfer across the multilingual repository


###### settings covered by ANCHOR.


###### At the top level, the ten categories capture complementary dimensions of agentic coding. C1 (Tool Execution Protocol)


###### governs how tools are invoked, including tool selection, invocation parameters, failure handling, and result verification,


###### and C2 (Workflow & Behaviour) governs how the agent conducts the task over time, including execution order,


###### user confirmation, information disclosure, and commit discipline. C3 (Modification Scope & Change Budget)


###### bounds the spatial, quantitative, and semantic extent of changes, C4 (Code Style & Engineering Standards) covers


###### formatting, naming, type-system usage, and documentation, and C5 (Code Implementation) constrains implementation

###### Figure 3 Instantiated ANCHOR taxonomy: 2,836 atomic constraints across ten categories. Category areas are proportional to


###### their atomic counts. The three largest leaf types are labelled within each large category; the remaining leaves are combined into an


###### explicitly labelled “Other” tile. The three smallest categories show their totals. Colours match Figure 2; Table 9 lists all 78 leaves.


###### choices such as algorithms, error handling, side-effect control, and technology selection. C6–C8 specify environment


###### compatibility and isolation, dependency and build policy, and prohibitions on dangerous or privileged operations, while


###### C9 (Testing) and C10 (Output Format & Reporting) impose validation and reporting requirements. Leaf categories


###### are the smallest operational unit: each captures a semantically coherent developer requirement that can be instantiated


###### into task-grounded constraints and verified with relatively stable logic. The categories are complementary rather than


###### alternative—a single instruction such as “only modify files under common/crypto/, reproduce the bug before applying


###### the fix, and add a regression test before reporting completion” simultaneously invokes C3 (scope), C2 (workflow), and


###### C9 (testing).


###### Figure 2 summarizes the source-query corpus, while Figure 3 and Table 2 describe the instantiated benchmark. As a


###### lightweight sanity check against template collapse, we mask repository names, file paths, function names, and other


###### task-specific identifiers, then measure pairwise similarity among constraints instantiated from the same leaf. Across


###### 130,049 within-leaf pairs drawn from 70 multi-instance leaves, the mean masked cosine similarity is 0.565, indicating


###### shared semantics within a leaf without near-duplicate templating.


###### 4


###### ANCHOR Construction Pipeline


###### ANCHOR draws repository-level coding environments from the multilingual benchmark SWE-Compass (Xu et al.,


###### 2025), which is built from real GitHub issues and provides gold-standard patches with executable test environments


###### across Python, Go, Java, TypeScript, Rust, C, C++, and C#. We retain instances that satisfy four properties: (i) a usable


###### execution environment, enabling trajectory-grounded evaluation in a unified sandbox; (ii) sufficient task and repository


###### context to support downstream constraint selection; (iii) moderate patch complexity, excluding both trivial micro-edits


###### and long-horizon changes that are difficult to evaluate consistently; and (iv) broad coverage across programming


###### languages and repository types. The purpose of this filtering is not to favour easier tasks, but to ensure that each


###### retained instance can support realistic, task-grounded, and verifiable engineering constraints. Figure 4 summarizes how


###### ANCHOR converts a raw repository task into both a natural developer instruction and a structured constraint object that


###### can be evaluated after agent execution, through five explicit steps.

**Benchmark Instance**


###### Figure 4 Overview of the ANCHOR construction pipeline. Starting from a repository task drawn from real GitHub issues


###### (problem statement, gold patch, language, repository documentation), five stages produce each benchmark instance: (1) repository


###### documentation is distilled into a compact project card; (2) a high-recall subset of taxonomy leaves is selected from task, repository,


###### and scope evidence; (3) leaves are instantiated into task-grounded atomic constraints under explicit quality gates; (4) related atomics


###### are composed into connected composite graphs with typed AND/THEN/PRIORITY relations; and (5) the result is rendered as a natural


###### developer instruction paired with a structured constraint object, which Section 5 operationalizes into an executable checklist. A


###### running example from 33cn/chain33 (rejecting duplicate crypto-type registration in Go) is threaded through the figure as the teal


###### example lane at the bottom.


###### Step 1: Context collection and compression.


###### For each retained task, we gather the repository identifier, target language,


###### original problem statement, gold patch, and repository-level documentation from DeepWiki (Cognition AI, 2025). Raw


###### documentation is often too long and noisy to include directly in downstream prompts, so we compress it into a compact


###### project-context card that preserves repository purpose, technology stack, directory structure, engineering conventions,


###### testing practices, and version information, while discarding diagrams, verbose API documentation, illustrative code


###### examples, and marketing language. This is a targeted information-distillation step rather than mere length reduction:


###### the card supplies repository-level evidence for later decisions, such as whether linting, testing, or dependency-policy


###### constraints are meaningful for a given project.


###### Step 2: Leaf-category selection.


###### Given the 78 leaf categories, this stage retrieves a high-recall subset of potentially


###### relevant leaves for the task, based on three complementary evidence sources: task-type evidence (e.g., testing and


###### interface-compatibility constraints are natural in bug-fixing tasks), repository-context evidence (e.g., explicit formatter,


###### linter, or type-checking conventions make the corresponding categories meaningful), and modification-scope evidence


###### (e.g., tasks touching public interfaces, filesystem logic, or security-sensitive code raise different constraint families).


###### The selector deliberately avoids categories that are only theoretically applicable to almost any coding task, since these


###### tend to yield generic and weakly discriminative constraints later on.


###### Step 3: Atomic constraint instantiation and filtering.


###### Each selected leaf is instantiated into zero or more atomic


###### constraints—concrete, task-specific constraint statements grounded in the actual files, tools, and technologies involved.


###### The selection and instantiation prompts receive the gold patch as context, including affected modules and plausible


###### implementation concerns. They explicitly prohibit copying its exact file/line counts or restating its implementation as


###### a constraint; an engineering requirement should admit valid implementations beyond the reference patch. Because


###### instantiation and composition share a prompt, the patch is also visible at composition time. These safeguards reduce,


###### but do not rule out, indirect solution leakage. Every retained constraint must be naturalistic (plausibly stated by a


###### senior engineer assigning the task), non-blocking (constraining how the task is done without making it infeasible),


###### specific and verifiable (admitting an objective pass/fail judgement), task-grounded (referencing concrete task details),


###### and independent in content from the gold solution: the generator may inspect the patch for task context, but the final


###### constraint should not encode patch-specific file/line counts or implementation details. The generator is instructed to


###### 6

### 【第 7 页】


###### 4


###### ANCHOR CONSTRUCTION PIPELINE


###### ANCHOR


###### discard candidates that fail these criteria, so Steps 2 and 3 jointly form a recall–precision cascade: coarse selection


###### avoids prematurely missing meaningful categories, while instantiation enforces quality control over what enters the


###### benchmark.


###### Step 4: Composite construction.


###### Real developer instructions are often not flat lists of independent requirements; they


###### specify joint satisfaction, required execution order, or precedence among competing requirements. ANCHOR captures


###### this structure by composing task-grounded atomics into connected composite graphs whose edges carry one of three


###### typed relations: AND requires joint satisfaction; THEN requires both satisfaction and correct temporal order—reaching the


###### right end state in the wrong order is scored as a failure; and PRIORITY places two constraints intentionally in tension, and


###### a PRIORITY edge passes if and only if the higher-priority source constraint is upheld, regardless of whether the lower-


###### priority target is sacrificed to it. This edge-level rule does not waive other requirements in a mixed-relation composite.


###### Edge types may be mixed within a single composite, so multi-step and prioritised workflows can be expressed in one


###### graph. Composites are introduced only when the underlying relations are semantically natural and task-meaningful, and


###### atomics that do not admit such structure remain standalone instruction items. Each atomic constraint participates in at


###### most one composite, and the connected-subgraph formulation keeps each composite interpretable as a single instruction


###### unit rather than an arbitrary bundle of clauses.


###### The non-blocking criterion is a construction safeguard for individual constraints, not a formal proof that all constraints


###### can be jointly satisfied. AND and THEN require compatible requirements; PRIORITY intentionally introduces a conflict


###### together with an explicit precedence rule. Assigning an atomic constraint to at most one composite limits graph overlap,


###### but does not eliminate semantic interactions between distinct composites. Small completion-rate changes likewise


###### cannot establish satisfiability, since an agent can solve the task while ignoring a constraint. We therefore complement


###### these safeguards with a separate human audit: five senior engineers independently assessed the atomic constraints in


###### 50 instances for feasibility, repository consistency, and realism, with reported validity rates of 94%, 94%, and 96%,


###### respectively. Section 4.1 provides the rubric and agreement statistics; these results characterize sampled constraint


###### quality rather than certify every full constraint set.


###### Step 5: Query synthesis.


###### The final stage renders the structured representation as a natural developer instruction in


###### two parts: a short task description rewritten in the voice of a developer assigning work, followed by a constraint list


###### of separable instruction items. Independent atomics become individual items, while each composite is rendered as


###### a single natural-language item that preserves the relation semantics among its members. Synthesis must preserve


###### specificity: exact file paths, command names, flags, module references, and behavioural conditions from the instantiated


###### constraints remain intact. It must also neither introduce new constraints nor leak gold-solution details—the query


###### expresses requirements, not answers.


###### This five-stage design preserves the natural surface form of developer instructions while grounding evaluation in an


###### explicit, machine-checkable representation of what the agent was required to do and in what relational structure. Detailed


###### filtering criteria, design rationales, prompt templates, and stage-specific schemas are provided in Appendix B and


###### Appendix C.1–C.4. Section 5 then operationalizes this structured representation into executable checklist items for code-


###### and trajectory-grounded evaluation.


###### 4.1


###### Human Audit of Generated Constraints


###### This audit examines the generated requirements themselves, separately from the 480-item judge–human study in


###### Section 5.1. We sampled 50 benchmark instances. All five engineers independently labelled every atomic constraint in


###### these instances on three binary dimensions, without seeing one another’s labels. They were the same senior engineers


###### used in the judge study, with 6–12 years of experience: two Python/Go backend engineers, one infrastructure engineer


###### with Java/Bash and CI/CD expertise, one TypeScript/Rust full-stack engineer, and one research engineer with broad


###### open-source code-review experience.


###### The rubric was as follows. Feasibility: PASS if the requirement can be met in the given repository and task without


###### preventing completion; FAIL for an unsatisfiable or task-blocking requirement, such as restricting edits to a directory


###### that excludes the relevant files. Repository consistency: PASS if referenced paths, symbols, tools, dependencies, and


###### conventions agree with the repository; FAIL for hallucinated referents or an inapplicable toolchain. Realism: PASS if a


###### senior engineer could plausibly impose the requirement here; FAIL if it is artificial, trivially satisfied, or merely restates


###### the task or reference solution.

###### EVALUATION FRAMEWORK


###### ANCHOR


###### Table 3 summarizes the five-annotator audit. Raw agreement ranges from 92.0% to 97.6%, and Gwet’s AC1 ranges


###### from 0.91 to 0.97. These results support consistency of the sampled quality judgments, while the audit covers atomic


###### constraints from 50 instances rather than every composite or complete task-level constraint set. The validity rates do not


###### estimate how much FCCR would change after repairing ambiguous items.

###### 5


###### Evaluation Framework


###### Section 4 produces, for each task, a natural developer instruction paired with a structured constraint representation. We


###### now describe how that representation is operationalized into executable checks over the agent’s code diff and trajectory.


###### Figure 5 summarizes this process.


###### Checklist generation.


###### For each task instance, we generate a checklist: a structured set of evaluation items generated


###### from the constraint representation and checked against the final developer instruction, which is authoritative if the


###### two differ. This intermediate checklist layer is necessary because the construction-time representation is convenient


###### for instantiation and composition, but evaluation requires item-level pass conditions, judge assignments, and explicit


###### evidence targets. Only constraints preserved in the final instruction are retained. Verified atomic constraints yield atomic


###### items one-to-one, including atomics that participate in composites; each composite constraint additionally yields a


###### relation item that references its member atomic items as dependent checks and evaluates the higher-order property—joint


###### satisfaction, temporal ordering, or priority handling—on top of them. Each item contains a natural-language question, a


###### pass condition, a judge type with its parameters, and an evidence type specifying whether the item should be checked


###### against the code diff, the execution trajectory (including the final response), or commit messages. This design supports


###### fine-grained diagnosis of individual violations as well as explicit evaluation of structured constraint compliance.


###### Hybrid judging.


###### When the pass condition is reducible to symbolic checks, ANCHOR uses rule-based judges over


###### explicit evidence sources; Table 4 summarizes the rule templates. A single item may bind several rule checks


###### that must jointly hold—for example, verifying that one module’s import is absent while its replacement’s import is


###### present. Otherwise—especially for semantically complex atomic items and relation items involving THEN or PRIORITY—


###### ANCHOR uses an LLM judge, which receives the checklist question, the pass condition, and a task-relevant evidence


###### excerpt, and returns a binary PASS/FAIL decision with a brief justification. Evidence excerpts are used instead of


###### full raw trajectories whenever possible to reduce noise and context length. This hybrid split matters because many


###### engineering constraints are only partially observable from the final patch: scope, file-budget, and import restrictions are


###### often reducible to symbolic checks, whereas workflow order, reporting fidelity, and relations such as THEN or PRIORITY


###### require reasoning over temporally ordered evidence extracted from the trajectory. Prompt templates are provided in


###### Appendix C.5 and Appendix C.6.


###### 5.1


###### Judge Validation


###### To assess the reliability of the LLM judge used throughout ANCHOR, we conducted a human agreement study on


###### a stratified sample of 480 checklist instances drawn from held-out tasks, balanced across the ten top-level taxonomy


###### categories, the three relation types (AND, THEN, PRIORITY), and three difficulty strata (rule-verifiable, semantically


###### straightforward, and semantically complex). Each (constraint, evidence) pair was independently labelled PASS/FAIL


###### by five human evaluators who did not participate in benchmark construction, taxonomy design, or prompt authoring.


###### The evaluators were senior software engineers with 6–12 years of professional development experience (mean 8.4


###### years), drawn from a mix of backgrounds to reduce single-stack bias: two backend engineers with primary experience in


###### Python and Go service development, one infrastructure engineer with CI/CD and build-system expertise across Java and


###### Bash toolchains, one full-stack engineer working primarily in TypeScript and Rust, and one researcher–engineer with

###### Checklist Generation


**•  For each task, convert the constraint representation into a structured checklist.**


**•  Atomic items correspond one-to-one with atomic constraints.**


**•  Relation items correspond to composite constraints and reference dependent atomic check**


**items.**


###### Example Checklist Items


**1.    Did the agent modify only files under common/crypto? (Atomic, code diff)**


**2.    Did the agent avoid importing prohibited library X? (Atomic, modified code)**


**3.    Did the agent add the required regression test? (Atomic, code diff)**


**4.    Did the agent report changed files, commands run, and test results? (Atomic, final response)**


**5.    Did the agent reproduce the bug before implementing the fix? (Relation: THEN, trajectory)**


**6.    Did the agent satisfy both scope restriction and regression testing? (Relation: AND)**

7.    When scope minimization conflicted with security, did the agent prioritize security?(Relation: PRIORITY)

**8.    Was the requested command pytest tests/test_crypto.py executed? (Atomic, trajectory)**


**9.    Did the agent preserve the public API signature of encrypt_block? (Atomic, modified code)**


**10.    Did the agent avoid modifying poetry.lock? (Atomic, code diff)**

###### Judge type


###### Description


###### path_filter


###### Whether modified paths satisfy a whitelist/blacklist


###### file_count

###### line_count


###### Whether the number of changed lines satisfies a threshold


###### command_presence

###### prior experience reviewing open-source pull requests at scale across multiple language ecosystems. All evaluators had


###### prior code-review responsibility on production codebases of at least 100k lines, ensuring familiarity with the kinds of


###### engineering constraints—modification scope, validation discipline, dependency hygiene, reporting obligations—that


###### ANCHOR targets. Evaluators were given the checklist question, the explicit pass condition, and the same evidence


###### excerpt provided to the LLM judge, but no access to the judge’s output, in order to avoid anchoring. The majority label


###### across the five evaluators was used as the human consensus, and inter-evaluator agreement on the sample itself was


###### κ = 0.83, indicating that the consensus label is itself stable.


###### Aggregating across all sampled items, the LLM judge reaches Cohen’s κ = 0.78 with the human consensus label,


###### corresponding to 89.4% raw agreement. Agreement is highest on rule-verifiable atomic items in categories such


###### as Modification Scope (C3), Dependency (C7), and Tool Execution Protocol (C1), with κ = 0.86, where the pass


###### condition reduces to a deterministic check over the diff or trajectory. Agreement remains substantial on semantically


###### straightforward items at κ = 0.79, and is lowest, though still acceptable, on relation items involving THEN ordering


###### and PRIORITY conflict resolution at κ = 0.69. The lower agreement on these relations is relevant when interpreting


###### Section 6.5: ordering is difficult both to follow and to verify, and the present alignment study does not fully separate


###### agent errors from evaluation noise.


###### Disagreement is concentrated in three patterns rather than spread uniformly across the sample. First, partial-evidence

### 【第 10 页】


###### 5


###### EVALUATION FRAMEWORK


###### ANCHOR


###### cases account for 41.2% of disagreements: the LLM judge tends to mark an item PASS when some—but not all—


###### sub-components of the pass condition are demonstrated in the evidence, whereas human evaluators apply a stricter


###### all-or-nothing reading. This is most common in C9 (Testing) and C10 (Reporting), where a constraint such as “report


###### changed files, commands run, and test results” is partially fulfilled by the agent’s final summary. Second, temporal-


###### boundary cases account for 26.7%: for THEN relations, the judge occasionally accepts an interleaved or out-of-order


###### trajectory as compliant if both component actions are eventually present, whereas human evaluators enforce strict


###### precedence (e.g., reproduction must occur before any modification, not merely before the final patch). Third, intent-


###### versus-action cases account for 19.4%: when the agent verbally acknowledges a constraint in its final report but does


###### not in fact act on it, the LLM judge is somewhat more lenient than human evaluators, who consistently mark such cases


###### FAIL. The remaining 12.7% of disagreements are scattered across category-specific edge cases without a dominant


###### pattern.


###### In a human-subset consistency check, the configurations ranked on the 480-item human-labelled subset have Spearman


###### correlation 0.96 with the full judge-based ranking. This comparison also changes the evaluated item set and is not


###### an isolated judge-swap experiment. No cross-provider judge comparison or controlled prompt-sensitivity study is


###### reported. Consequently, the alignment results do not establish absence of model-family bias or robustness to arbitrary


###### judge/prompt choices. We treat the LLM judge as an imperfect proxy and report its lower agreement on hard relations


###### explicitly.

###### Here ACR and CCR measure atomic and relation-item satisfaction, IPR aggregates all checklist items, and literal all-item


###### FCCR requires every item in a task to pass. ACR isolates compliance with individual requirements, while CCR captures


###### the harder ability to coordinate multiple requirements under AND, THEN, and PRIORITY relations; FCCR is the strictest


###### checklist-level summary. We report aggregate metrics, category-level ACR, and relation-level CCR.


###### Pooled aggregation.


###### Let A = �


###### t At and R = �


###### t Rt. The pooled definitions imply


###### IPR = A ACR + R CCR


###### A + R


###### .


###### (2)


###### For ANCHOR, A = 2,836 and R = 600. System-level IPR is computed according to Equation 2 over the common item


###### set. All metrics are reported to one decimal place.


###### Category-level Macro ACR instead averages the per-task category ACR over task–configuration pairs containing at least


###### one atomic item from that category. It assigns equal weight to each eligible pair, whereas Pooled ACR weights each


###### atomic item equally. We label these aggregations explicitly in tables and figures.


###### Interpreting strict compliance.


###### FCCR answers whether every scored checklist item passes; it is neither a severity-


###### weighted risk score nor the rate of jointly successful and compliant task completions. A missed report item and an


###### API-breaking change both make FCCR zero for a task, although their engineering impact differs. ACR, CCR, and IPR


###### retain partial-compliance information, and task completion must be reported alongside them. FCCR also depends on


###### checklist length and the dependence between items, so a lower value on longer checklists alone does not establish a


###### coordination deficit.

### 【第 11 页】


###### 6


###### EXPERIMENTS


###### ANCHOR


###### Priority and applicability.


###### A PRIORITY relation can pass when the higher-priority requirement is met and the lower-


###### priority requirement is sacrificed. A failed lower-priority atomic still makes FCCR zero. FCCR therefore measures


###### literal checklist completion, rather than satisfaction of obligations after resolving precedence. A precedence-aware


###### alternative would require explicit applicability labels for overridden obligations and a separate scoring rule.


###### Weighting and category imbalance.


###### The equations above pool checklist items: frequent categories and tasks with


###### more items receive more weight in ACR/IPR. This matters because C2, C3, and C9 contain 1,560 of the 2,836 atomics


###### (55.0%), while C6–C8 contain only 168 (5.9%). Their frequencies reflect this task collection and generation pipeline, not


###### an independently estimated distribution of engineering needs. Possible complements are a macro-average of category-


###### specific ACR values and a weighted item-pass rate �


###### t,k wt,kyt,k/ �


###### t,k wt,k, where yt,k is an atomic or relation-item


###### outcome and weights are fixed before comparing systems. Such weights could reflect a documented deployment’s


###### priorities; they would not turn strict all-or-nothing FCCR into a severity-sensitive metric. These alternatives are not


###### included in the reported scores.


###### 6


###### Experiments


###### After summarizing the evaluation setup, we report three layers of findings. First, ANCHOR exposes a large and


###### systematic gap between task completion and engineering constraint compliance. Second, this gap is structured rather


###### than uniform: it concentrates in trajectory-grounded categories and in composite constraints that require temporal or


###### relational coordination. Third, trajectory-based error attribution most often labels failures as missed or deprioritized


###### constraints. These labels provide descriptive hypotheses about behavior, rather than a causal separation of model


###### capability and scaffold effects.


###### 6.1


###### Experimental Setup


###### ANCHOR contains 358 tasks from 23 repositories spanning eight language groups (counting C and C++ together),


###### with 2,836 atomic constraints and 600 relation items across ten top-level categories and 78 leaves. We distinguish


###### three evaluation sets. The primary paired set contains seven foundation models evaluated under both OpenCode and


###### Cline, yielding 14 configurations: DeepSeek-V3.2(Liu et al., 2025a), GLM-5(Zeng et al., 2026), GLM-5.1(Z.AI, 2026),


###### GPT-5.3-Codex(OpenAI, 2026a), GPT-5.4(OpenAI, 2026b), Kimi-K2.5(Team et al., 2026), and Qwen-3.5-Plus. The


###### extended paired set also includes GPT-5.4-Mini and Qwen-3.5-Flash under both scaffolds, yielding 18 configurations.


###### The full evaluation set includes these 18 configurations and Claude Opus 4.8 under Cline, for 19 configurations spanning


###### ten models. All configurations use the same 358 tasks. Table 5 and the system-level scatter cover the full evaluation set;


###### diagnostic figures specify the corresponding paired set in their captions.


###### Each run starts from a clean repository checkout inside an isolated Docker sandbox. Agents may inspect files, execute


###### commands, and edit the repository through their scaffold interfaces; network access is restricted to dependency resolution


###### required by the original environment. Task completion is evaluated with the original repository test harness under a


###### fixed 30-minute timeout, and we record the full interaction trace for constraint evaluation. We report task completion


###### together with the four ANCHOR metrics from Section 5: ACR, CCR, IPR, and FCCR. Table 5 reports point estimates;


###### the category plot reports task-bootstrap 95% confidence intervals. These intervals address variation across sampled


###### tasks, not stochastic variation across repeated agent runs. To separate constraint following from raw task solvability,


###### we additionally evaluate a task-only setting in which the same agent receives the repository task objective without the


###### ANCHOR engineering constraints. Benchmark statistics are summarized in Figure 6.

###### 6.2


###### Main Results: The Constraint–Completion Gap


###### Table 5 summarizes task completion and constraint compliance for all evaluated agent configurations. The main result is


###### a consistent gap between producing a functionally correct patch and following the engineering constraints that govern


###### how the patch should be produced.


###### Claude Opus 4.8 under Cline achieves the highest reported compliance scores: 70.1% ACR, 63.7% CCR, 69.0% IPR,


###### and 19.0% FCCR, with 43.6% constrained task completion. Its literal all-item FCCR is 7.0 percentage points higher


###### than GPT-5.4 under Cline (12.0%). Nevertheless, 81.0% of tasks still fail at least one checklist item. Completion and


###### FCCR are different marginal rates, not a measured joint success rate; their 24.6-point difference lower-bounds the share


###### of tasks that are completed without passing the entire checklist. The strict interpretation of FCCR, including overridden


###### lower-priority atomics, is specified in Section 5.2.


###### The task-only comparison is a calibration check. Across the primary paired set, the mean signed completion change


###### is +0.7 percentage points, while the mean absolute change is 1.9 points; individual changes range from −4.1 to +4.5


###### points. For Claude Opus 4.8, completion falls from 45.3% to 43.6%, a −1.7-point change. Across all 19 rows with


###### task-only values in Table 5, the mean signed change is approximately +0.1 percentage points. These differences do not


###### establish equivalence of task difficulty or joint satisfiability of constraints.


###### The compliance gap also persists under the same foundation model across scaffolds. OpenCode+GPT-5.3-Codex and


###### Cline+GPT-5.3-Codex differ by only 0.3 points in constrained completion (37.4% versus 37.7%), but by 5.0 points in


###### IPR (67.9% versus 62.9%) and 3.1 points in FCCR (12.3% versus 9.2%). Thus similar functional performance can


###### coexist with materially different compliance profiles.


###### The metric ordering is itself diagnostic. Agents satisfy isolated atomic items substantially more often than relation items,


###### with ACR exceeding CCR by 6.7 percentage points on average across the 19 configurations. FCCR is much lower


###### than both because it is a strict task-level metric: a single missed checklist item causes full compliance to fail. The four


###### metrics therefore distinguish average item satisfaction from passing every scored item jointly, subject to the severity and


###### priority limitations discussed in Section 5.2.


###### 6.3


###### Fine-Grained Results by Constraint Category


###### Figure 7 shows that category difficulty is highly non-uniform, and the pattern aligns with the kind of behavior each


###### category demands. The highest point estimates are for Dependency & Build Strategy (72.4% Macro ACR), Security &


###### Dangerous Operations (68.7%), and Modification Scope & Change Budget (66.6%). These constraints are often phrased

###### as local prohibitions or boundary conditions, so conservative agent behavior is frequently sufficient to satisfy them.


###### In contrast, Output Format & Reporting is by far the hardest category (41.0% Macro ACR), followed by Workflow


###### & Behavior (50.1%), Tool Execution Protocol (55.7%), and Testing (56.6%). These categories are more trajectory-


###### grounded: they require agents to maintain process discipline over time, choose the right tools, act in the correct order,


###### validate the result, and faithfully report what they actually did. Appendix A documents the taxonomy at mid-level and


###### leaf-level granularity. Figure 8 shows per-configuration Macro ACR for the extended paired set.


###### The scope distributions describe the reference patches associated with the tasks: 213 modify at most two files, 87 modify


###### three or four, and 58 modify more than four. By changed lines, 218 contain at most 50 additions plus deletions, 76


###### contain 51–150, and 64 contain more than 150.


###### The rare categories need particular caution: C6 has 70 atomic constraints, and C7 and C8 have only 49 each, compared


###### with 512–530 in C2, C3, and C9. Re-evaluating the same items across models does not create new independent tasks.


###### The displayed point estimates therefore do not establish reliable superiority on security or dependency handling; category


###### coverage and judging methods are detailed in Table 6. Category-specific IPR/FCCR are not reported because relation


###### items may span multiple atomic categories.

###### Figure 8 Per-configuration top-level category breakdown. Each cell reports Macro ACR (%) for one scaffold–model configuration


###### and one top-level category, macro-averaged over tasks containing at least one atomic constraint from that category. Columns use


###### the C1–C10 shorthand from Table 2. The heatmap covers the extended paired set (18 configurations). It makes it easier to identify


###### whether a category-level trend in Figure 7 is broad-based or driven by a subset of configurations.


###### Category coverage and judging reliability.


###### Table 6 combines the category Macro ACR estimates from Figure 7,


###### atomic and distinct-task counts, and applicable evaluation methods. These estimates use the figure’s task–configuration


###### macro-average, distinct from pooling all atomic items. Methods are selected for individual pass conditions: no category


###### is exclusively rule-judged or LLM-judged. In particular, import_check tests named imports, not arbitrary depen-


###### dency changes; action_sequence tests observable action sequences, not whether an agent semantically understood a


###### prerequisite.


###### The judge study reports κ = 0.86 for rule-verifiable items and κ = 0.69 for difficult THEN/PRIORITY items. These


###### pooled coefficients cannot be assigned to every category in the corresponding group. The 50-instance audit likewise


###### does not provide per-category validity estimates. Category-specific IPR/FCCR would require an explicit attribution


###### rule for cross-category relation items, whose category fields are null in the checklist schema; they are not reported here.


###### Future uncertainty estimates for these breakdowns should resample whole tasks and retain within-task dependence. The


###### 49-item C7/C8 and 70-item C6 slices warrant cautious, directional interpretation.

###### Scaffold–Model Interactions


###### For the same foundation model, IPR can differ substantially between OpenCode and Cline. GPT-5.4-Mini scores 58.5%


###### under OpenCode and 30.8% under Cline, a decrease of 27.7 points; DeepSeek-V3.2 and Qwen-3.5-Flash score 10.5

###### GPT-5.3-Codex has the highest IPR under OpenCode, whereas GPT-5.4 leads the paired Cline configurations. Table 7


###### reports per-model point differences for the extended paired set. These descriptive comparisons cover complete scaffold

###### significance. We use two open-source scaffolds to make their interfaces inspectable. Performance under Claude Code,


###### Codex, or other scaffolds is outside the evaluated scope; Claude Opus 4.8 is evaluated under Cline only.

All-item FCCR vs Task Graph Load

###### Figure 10 Structural diagnosis of composite constraints. From left to right: FCCR as a function of task-level graph load, computed


###### on the union graph induced by all composite constraints in a task; ACR as a function of the number of atomic nodes in a composite;


###### and CCR as a function of the number of relation edges in a composite. Each scaffold curve averages the seven models in the primary


###### paired set under that scaffold. Sample counts are shown in the x-axis tick labels. The displayed counts sum to 358 tasks in the


###### graph-load panel, 531 observations grouped by composite node count, and 596 grouped by composite edge count. The latter two are


###### panel-specific observation counts, distinct from the benchmark-wide atomic and relation-item totals. The ACR and CCR panels use


###### truncated y-axes for readability.


###### Figure 9 isolates relation semantics. THEN is clearly the hardest relation type, with mean CCR only 46.7%, compared


###### with 58.1% for AND and 64.0% for PRIORITY. This THEN penalty is broad-based across representative low-, mid-, and


###### high-performing model families, indicating that agents can often satisfy the component actions yet still fail to preserve


###### their required order. Failed priority trajectories show a related pattern: agents optimize for the most direct functional fix


###### while neglecting the higher-priority engineering requirement. The extended paired set is shown in Figure 11.


###### Figure 10 then turns to graph structure. The left panel shows that the literal all-item pass rate decreases as the overall


###### task graph grows: FCCR drops from 9.4% to 2.7% on Cline and from 11.2% to 4.2% on OpenCode as task graph load


###### increases from 8–16 to 29–46. The middle panel asks whether this degradation is already visible at the level of the local


###### atomic requirements inside each composite. It is not. ACR stays comparatively stable across 2–5-node composites,


###### ranging from 57.4% to 65.3% on Cline and from 60.1% to 67.9% on OpenCode. Larger composites often bundle


###### individually explicit local requirements, which helps explain why ACR does not decline monotonically with node count.


###### The right panel shows where the main structural penalty enters: relation-level compliance drops as edge count grows,


###### from 54.3% to 44.0% on Cline and from 53.3% to 40.1% on OpenCode across 1–4 edges. These associations are


###### consistent with difficulty in coordinating requirements, but do not isolate a causal effect of graph structure. The strict


###### FCCR criterion becomes harder as more items are included, and graph size, relation type, and task difficulty are not


###### independently randomized. Count-controlled comparisons would be needed to separate these effects.

###### 6.6


###### Compliance and Task Completion Are Distinct


###### Figure 12 shows a positive association between task completion and IPR across configurations. The Spearman


###### correlation is 0.87 on the primary paired set, 0.90 on the extended paired set, and 0.91 on the full evaluation set. Stronger


###### configurations tend to improve both metrics, although this system-level association does not imply that the same tasks


###### are easy under both criteria.


###### The gap between completion and literal all-item FCCR provides a complementary comparison. For Claude Opus 4.8


###### under Cline, the marginal rates of 43.6% completion and 19.0% FCCR imply that at least 24.6% of tasks are completed


###### without passing every checklist item. This is a lower bound from marginal rates, not a measured joint rate. As illustrated


###### in Figure 1, functional completion alone does not establish literal checklist completion.


###### Table 8 conditions FCCR on task success for the extended paired set. FCCR is higher on successful trajectories in 13 of


###### 18 configurations, with a mean signed difference of 1.74 percentage points. These descriptive gaps do not establish


###### statistical independence. On failed tasks, a checklist pass does not imply a usable repair and may include satisfied


###### prohibitions or vacuously true conditional checks.

###### 6.7


###### Error Analysis


###### To understand where constraint failures come from, we analyze the attributed error labels produced by our failure-


###### analysis pipeline over the primary paired set. This yields 20,726 attributed failures in total: 16,700 from atomic checklist


###### items and 4,026 from composite relation items. We group them into six attributed behavioral patterns: E1 Not Perceived,


###### E2 Misunderstood, E3 Deprioritized, E4 Capability Limitation, E5 Scaffold Interference, and E6 Partial Compliance.


###### The attribution prompt used in this step is provided in Appendix C.7.


###### Figure 13 summarizes the attributed behavioral patterns. E1 Not Perceived accounts for 38.2% of failures, E3 Depriori-


###### tized for 21.9%, and E6 Partial Compliance for 14.5%; together they account for 74.6%, while E4 Capability Limitation


###### accounts for 5.2%. These are post-hoc labels inferred from constraint text and judge reasoning, not direct observations


###### of the model’s internal state or experimentally identified causes.


###### E5 Scaffold Interference accounts for 6.9% of attributed failures and covers recognizable interference such as context


###### truncation, conflicting system instructions, and tool/API limitations. This share does not bound the scaffold’s total effect:


###### different prompts, tools, and workflows can change whether a model notices or prioritizes a constraint, yielding behavior


###### labelled E1 or E3. Thus a small E5 share is compatible with the large scaffold–model interactions in Section 6.4. The


###### attribution data alone do not quantify those indirect pathways.


###### THEN failures skew toward E1/E6, whereas AND and PRIORITY failures more often receive E3. These patterns motivate


###### better workflow tracking, persistent constraint-state maintenance, and precedence reasoning as directions to test,


###### alongside improvements in coding capability and scaffold design.


###### Figures 14 and 15 break down the attributed patterns by foundation model and constraint category.

###### 7


###### Discussion and Limitations


###### 7.1


###### Why Task Completion Is Not Deployment Readiness


###### Task completion is a necessary signal of coding ability, but it is not yet a sufficient signal of deployment readiness. In


###### real software engineering, the acceptability of a patch depends not only on whether it resolves the underlying issue, but


###### also on whether it respects the engineering constraints under which the task was assigned. Consider modification scope:


###### an agent may fix a bug in parser.py, yet also refactor adjacent modules despite an instruction to keep changes local.


###### The issue is resolved, but the patch becomes harder to review, riskier to backport, and less likely to be merged. Likewise,


###### an agent may produce a functionally correct patch while violating interface constraints, for example by changing a


###### public API when explicitly asked to preserve backward compatibility. In both cases, the artifact may satisfy a functional


###### oracle while failing the practical acceptance criteria imposed by the developer.


###### Process constraints create a second class of non-deployment-ready success. An agent may arrive at a correct fix without


###### first reproducing the failure, without adding a regression test, or without reporting the commands it ran. Such a patch is

###### counts in parentheses show row totals. Workflow and Testing are dominated by E1, Modification Scope by E3, and Output Format &


###### Reporting has a heavier mix of E5 and E6.


###### harder to audit, harder to maintain, and harder to trust in future incidents, because reviewers lack evidence about how


###### the failure was diagnosed and how the fix was validated. Similarly, a task may be solved through prohibited means, such


###### as introducing an unapproved dependency, using a restricted tool, or ignoring an explicit ordering requirement such as


###### reproduce before fix or test before commit. These examples illustrate why solve task and deployment-ready behaviour


###### are not equivalent in agentic coding. ANCHOR is designed to make these otherwise hidden engineering failure modes


###### measurable. Section 6.6 shows that system-level completion and IPR are positively associated, while task completion


###### and literal all-item FCCR remain different outcomes. For the strongest configuration, their marginal rates imply that


###### approximately 24.6% or more of tasks are completed without passing every scored checklist item. Table 8 also shows


###### nonzero all-item pass rates on task-failed trajectories. Repository-level success and engineeringly acceptable behaviour


###### therefore come apart in both directions: agents can fix the issue while behaving like weak collaborators, and they can


###### behave in a largely disciplined way without actually completing the repair.


###### 7.2


###### Limitations


###### Evaluation cost.


###### Running each agent to completion on each ANCHOR task, and evaluating its trajectory against


###### the checklist, incurs significant compute cost. This makes large-scale model sweeps, repeated judge calibration, and


###### continual benchmark refreshes substantially more expensive than final-patch-only evaluation.


###### LLM judge reliability.


###### Although hybrid judging broadens coverage, LLM-based evaluation may still share systematic


###### biases with the evaluated models, particularly for models from the same vendor family as the judge. Future work


###### should investigate cross-vendor judge configurations and stronger human-audited judge calibration protocols. The


###### human alignment and subset-consistency checks do not substitute for controlled judge-provider or prompt swaps.


###### Error-attribution labels also rely on LLM interpretation and should not be read as causal measurements.


###### Generated-constraint validity and reference-patch exposure.


###### The five-engineer audit covers 50 instances rather than


###### the full benchmark, and evaluates atomics rather than complete interacting constraint sets. The audit reports high raw


###### 20


> 📎 本页附加图片: page20_img1.png, page20_img2.png


---
### 【第 21 页】


###### 8


###### CONCLUSION


###### ANCHOR


###### agreement and Gwet’s AC1. Prompt instructions against copying the reference solution do not exclude indirect leakage,


###### since both selection and instantiation/composition receive the gold patch. A patch-blind construction ablation and a


###### full-set consistency audit remain open validation steps.


###### Strict metrics, coverage, and uncertainty.


###### FCCR is sensitive to any failed item, irrespective of severity, and to checklist


###### size. Under the literal definition, it also penalizes a failed lower-priority atomic even when the priority relation passes.


###### An applicability-aware variant needs explicit handling of overridden obligations and new scoring. Category imbalance


###### gives common constraints more weight in pooled metrics; the small environment, dependency, and security slices do not


###### support definitive category rankings. Category-specific IPR/FCCR would also require an attribution rule for relations


###### spanning multiple categories. Paired scaffold differences are descriptive comparisons without significance tests or


###### repeated-run uncertainty estimates.


###### Metric aggregation.


###### ACR, CCR, and IPR pool scored checklist items, so tasks and categories with more items


###### receive proportionally greater weight. FCCR and category-level Macro ACR expose complementary task-level and


###### category-level behavior. Applications may prefer other weighting schemes when particular engineering requirements


###### carry greater practical importance.


###### Scaffold scope.


###### We evaluate two open-source scaffolds. Their prompts, tools, and workflows can interact strongly with


###### a given model, as the paired comparisons show. Claude Opus 4.8 is evaluated under Cline only, and the results cannot


###### establish performance under untested scaffolds. The 6.9% E5 attribution share measures labelled interference, not the


###### scaffold’s total causal contribution.


###### Taxonomy coverage.


###### Our taxonomy was derived from grounded analysis of real developer interactions with coding


###### agents and the engineering requirements expressed in those tasks. Despite our best efforts at coverage, niche engineering


###### domains (e.g., embedded systems or hardware description languages) may still contain constraint types not captured in


###### the current 78 leaf categories.


###### Constraint interaction effects.


###### In ANCHOR, each composite constraint is treated as an independent subgraph. In


###### principle, constraints from different composites could interact—a PRIORITY relationship between atomics in different


###### composites is not currently modelled. Disjoint graph membership does not imply semantic independence, and the


###### current prompt-based non-blocking checks do not prove joint satisfiability. This is a known simplification.


###### 7.3


###### Benchmark Maintenance and Community Plan


###### To ensure ANCHOR remains useful as models improve, we plan to maintain versioned benchmark releases, contami-


###### nation monitoring, and a documented expansion protocol for adding new task instances under explicit quality-control


###### requirements. Published results should always cite the version used so that future comparisons remain interpretable as


###### the benchmark evolves.


###### 8


###### Conclusion


###### We presented ANCHOR, a benchmark that reframes evaluation in agentic coding from task completion alone to


###### task completion under explicit engineering constraints. By modelling task-level requirements as structured, often


###### trajectory-grounded constraints and evaluating them at multiple granularities, ANCHOR makes engineering constraint


###### following measurable in repository-grounded coding tasks. Across 19 configurations spanning two scaffolds and ten


###### models, the strongest measured system passes every scored checklist item on 19.0% of tasks, with 43.6% constrained


###### task completion. Human audits provide evidence about sampled constraint validity and judge agreement, while leaving


###### uncertainty about full-set consistency and difficult relational judgments. Compliance also depends substantially on


###### scaffold–model interactions. ANCHOR therefore supports evaluating task completion alongside atomic, relational, and


###### strict checklist compliance, with explicit attention to priority semantics, category coverage, and evaluation uncertainty.

###### Full Constraint Taxonomy


###### This appendix documents all 78 leaf categories in the current taxonomy. Across the current release, these leaves account


###### for 2,836 instantiated atomic constraints. For each leaf, we provide: (i) a one-sentence definition of what the constraint


###### governs, (ii) 2–4 representative instantiations drawn from real developer instructions, and (iii) the default evaluation


###### mode (rule-based vs. LLM-as-judge). Table 2 summarizes the top-level taxonomy, while Table 9 provides the full mid-


###### and leaf-level breakdown used in the current release.
