###### B


###### Additional Methodological Details


###### B.1


###### Taxonomy Design Principles


###### The ANCHOR taxonomy was not designed purely from an a priori schema. Instead, we started from grounded open-label


###### annotation over real developer interactions with coding agents, including task instructions, inline review comments,


###### and post-hoc feedback on agent trajectories. Senior software engineers then iteratively clustered near-duplicate labels,


###### resolved boundary cases, and stress-tested the emerging hierarchy against held-out trajectories. The goal of this


###### refinement process was not merely descriptive coverage, but an operational taxonomy that could support scalable


###### instantiation, evaluation, and error analysis.


###### Five design principles guided this process. First, coverage: the taxonomy should cover the major engineering constraint


###### types that appear in practice, so that real developer instructions map into it with minimal residue. Second, semantic


###### exclusivity: leaf categories should be as disjoint as possible, reducing ambiguity during annotation and automatic


###### mapping. Third, operationalisability: each leaf should admit concrete, testable instantiations with stable pass conditions


###### 27


---
### 【第 28 页】


###### B


###### ADDITIONAL METHODOLOGICAL DETAILS


###### ANCHOR


###### under either rule-based or LLM-based evaluation. Fourth, hierarchical organisation: the taxonomy should support both


###### fine-grained diagnostic analysis at the leaf level and coarse-grained reporting at the category level. Fifth, language


###### neutrality: the same conceptual categories should transfer across the multilingual repository settings covered by


###### ANCHOR.


###### The current release instantiates this design as a three-level hierarchy with 10 top-level categories, 34 mid-level sub-


###### categories, and 78 leaf categories. At a coarse level, these groups capture complementary dimensions of engineering


###### behaviour: process- and trajectory-level constraints (C1–C2), patch- and implementation-level constraints (C3–C5),


###### environment and system-boundary constraints (C6–C8), and validation and reporting requirements (C9–C10). Leaf


###### categories are the smallest operational unit: each is intended to capture a semantically coherent developer requirement


###### that can be instantiated into task-grounded constraints and evaluated with relatively stable verification logic.


###### B.2


###### Environment Curation and Context Compression


###### The construction pipeline begins by selecting repository tasks that can support realistic and verifiable engineering


###### constraints. The filtering objective is not to privilege easier tasks, but to ensure that each retained instance admits


###### trajectory-grounded evaluation. In practice, we prioritise instances with (i) a usable execution environment, (ii) sufficient


###### task and repository context to support downstream constraint selection, (iii) moderate patch complexity rather than


###### trivial micro-edits or very long-horizon changes, and (iv) broad coverage across programming languages and repository


###### types.


###### For each retained instance, we collect the repository identifier, target language, original problem statement, gold patch,


###### and repository-level documentation from DeepWiki (Cognition AI, 2025). These artefacts provide complementary


###### signals for later stages: the problem statement and patch reveal the task objective and likely affected code regions, while


###### repository documentation provides project-level context such as architecture, tooling, testing practices, and engineering


###### conventions.


###### Raw repository documentation is often too long and noisy to include directly in downstream prompts. We therefore


###### compress it into a compact project-context card that preserves information useful for constraint construction, such as


###### repository purpose, technology stack, directory structure, testing practices, and version information, while discarding


###### low-value content such as diagrams, verbose API documentation, illustrative code examples, marketing language, and


###### redundant passages. This step is not merely length reduction: it is a targeted information-distillation stage that gives


###### later prompts repository-level evidence without overwhelming the context budget.


###### B.3


###### Subset Selection and Atomic Instantiation


###### Given the full taxonomy, the next challenge is deciding which leaf categories are relevant to a particular task. ANCHOR


###### treats this as a high-recall selection problem. The coarse selection stage receives the full taxonomy, the repository


###### identifier, programming language, problem statement, compressed repository context, and the gold patch. The prompt


###### permits the gold patch to inform task context and category selection, while explicitly forbidding requirements that copy


###### its exact counts or restate the reference solution. This is a prompt-level safeguard, not evidence that category selection is


###### independent of the patch.


###### Selection decisions are based on three complementary evidence sources. The first is task-type evidence: some categories


###### are more naturally associated with certain task types, such as testing or interface-compatibility constraints in bug-


###### fixing tasks. The second is repository-context evidence: formatter, linter, type-checking, or dependency-management


###### conventions make some categories more meaningful for a given project. The third is modification-scope evidence: tasks


###### touching public interfaces, filesystem logic, exception handling, or security-sensitive code naturally raise different


###### constraint families. At the same time, the selector avoids retaining categories that are only theoretically applicable to


###### almost any coding task, because these tend to yield generic and weakly discriminative constraints later on.


###### This stage is intentionally followed by an implicit precision filter during atomic instantiation. A selected category is


###### retained in the final benchmark instance only if it can yield one or more constraints that are naturalistic, non-blocking,


###### specific and verifiable, task-grounded, and independent of the reference implementation in content. Categories that


###### would only produce generic, overly restrictive, or weakly diagnostic constraints are dropped. In this sense, ANCHOR


###### uses a recall–precision cascade: coarse selection aims to avoid prematurely missing meaningful categories, while atomic


###### instantiation enforces quality control over what ultimately appears in the benchmark.


###### 28


---
### 【第 29 页】


###### C


###### PROMPT TEMPLATES


###### ANCHOR


###### B.4


###### Composite Constraints and Query Synthesis


###### Real developer instructions are often not flat lists of independent requirements. They may specify joint satisfaction,


###### required execution order, or precedence among competing requirements. ANCHOR captures this structure by organising


###### selected subsets of atomic constraints into connected composite graphs. Edges carry one of three typed relations: AND


###### requires joint satisfaction, THEN requires both satisfaction and correct temporal order, and PRIORITY requires the source


###### constraint to take precedence over the target when the two are in tension.


###### Composite constraints are introduced only when the underlying relations are semantically natural and task-meaningful;


###### ANCHOR does not force unrelated constraints into a shared graph merely to increase structural complexity. Not all


###### atomic constraints must participate in composites, and standalone constraints remain as independent instruction items.


###### Edge types may be mixed within one composite, and each atomic constraint participates in at most one composite,


###### so composites partition the related atomics into disjoint, self-contained instruction units. This connected-subgraph


###### formulation keeps each composite interpretable as a single instruction unit rather than an arbitrary bundle of checklist


###### clauses.


###### The final query-synthesis stage converts the structured representation into a natural developer instruction suitable for


###### direct agent execution. Each query is written in two parts: a short task description rewritten in the voice of a developer


###### assigning work, and a constraint list composed of separable instruction items. Independent atomic constraints become


###### individual items, while each composite constraint is rendered as a single natural-language item that preserves the relation


###### semantics among its member atomics. A key requirement of this stage is specificity preservation: exact file paths,


###### command names, flags, module references, and behavioural conditions from the instantiated constraints must remain


###### intact. The synthesis step must also avoid introducing new constraints or leaking gold-solution details. Prompt templates


###### for these stages are provided in Appendix C.1–C.4.


###### C


###### Prompt Templates


###### C.1


###### Context Compression Prompt


###### The released prompt directory does not contain the repository-context compression template. In the current pipeline,


###### this step uses a shorter summarization instruction to distill raw DeepWiki documentation into a compact project-context


###### card covering repository purpose, technology stack, directory structure, engineering conventions, testing practices, and


###### version compatibility. The prompt templates below are included verbatim from the released prompt set, with variable


###### slots preserved in braces.


###### C.2


###### Coarse Constraint Selection Prompt


###### Constraint subset selection prompt

You are an expert in software engineering practices and agentic coding evaluation. Your task is to analyze a coding task and
determine which engineering constraint categories are applicable to it.
## Context
We are building a benchmark to evaluate how well AI coding agents follow engineering constraints during agentic coding tasks. For
each coding task, we need to select which constraint categories are relevant, so that we can later generate specific
constraint instances for those categories.
## Constraint Taxonomy
Below is the complete taxonomy of engineering constraints in JSON format. Each leaf category (the deepest level with a "
description" field) is the finest granularity. Your job is to select which leaf categories are applicable to the given task.
{constraint_taxonomy}
## Input
You will receive the following information about a coding task:
### Repository
{repo}
### Programming Language
{programming_languages}

###### 29


---
### 【第 30 页】


###### C


###### PROMPT TEMPLATES


###### ANCHOR

### Problem Statement
{problem_statement}
### Patch
The following is the complete diff of the gold-standard fix for this task. Use it to understand what files are involved, what kind
of changes are made, and what code areas are affected. Do NOT use it to reverse-engineer constraints that exactly match the
patch (e.g., do not set the EXACT same count as the patch). Instead, use it to judge which categories of constraints would
be reasonable for this task.
{patch}
### Project Context (from DeepWiki)
The following is the DeepWiki documentation for this repository. It may be lengthy. Focus on extracting: (1) the project’s tech
stack and core dependencies, (2) module architecture and directory structure, (3) engineering practices if mentioned, (4)
infrastructure and external services. Ignore irrelevant sections.
{repo_context}
## Instructions
1. Read all the input information carefully. For long inputs (patch, DeepWiki), focus on extracting the key signals relevant to
constraint selection rather than reading every line in detail.
2. For each leaf category in the taxonomy, determine whether it is applicable to this task. A category is applicable if:
- The task’s nature makes this type of constraint meaningful (e.g., "Test Coverage Requirements" is applicable to a bug fix
task)
- The project’s tech stack or practices make this type of constraint relevant (e.g., "Type Safety Restrictions" is relevant for
a TypeScript project)
- The modification involved could reasonably be subject to this type of constraint (e.g., "Injection Prevention" is relevant if
the patch touches input handling code)
3. Do NOT select a category just because it could theoretically apply to any task. For example:
- "Platform Compatibility" should NOT be selected if the task is a simple Python logic fix with no file/path/encoding
operations
- "Performance Requirements" should NOT be selected if the task is a UI text change
- "Concurrency & Synchronization Strategy" should NOT be selected if the task has nothing to do with concurrent code
4. DO select categories that are clearly relevant even if the task description doesn’t explicitly mention them. For example:
- "Error Handling" should be selected if the patch modifies exception-handling code, even if the issue description doesn’t
mention error handling
- "Lint & Formatter Tools" should be selected if the project uses a known linter (visible in project context), even if not
mentioned in the issue
5. The following categories require special attention because they are often overlooked despite being broadly applicable to most
coding tasks. Evaluate each of them carefully before deciding to skip:
- **Tool Selection (1.1) and Call Parameters (1.2)**: Think from the USER’s perspective -- users don’t know the agent’s
internal tool names. They give commands like "use grep to search", "don’t use force push", "run tests with verbose output".
Select these categories when the task involves operations where a user might naturally specify preferred commands, forbidden
commands, or required/forbidden flags.
- **Commit Convention (2.4)**: Select this category for any task where the agent will produce code changes that should be
committed. Commit message format, commit granularity, and pre-commit checks are standard engineering practices. Do NOT skip
this category just because it "applies to any task" -- it has specific, instantiable conventions (Conventional Commits,
signed-off-by, scope tags, etc.) that vary by project.
- **Quantity Scope (3.2)**: File count and line count constraints are natural engineering guardrails (e.g., "keep this change
minimal", "don’t touch more than N files"). These are applicable to most code modification tasks. Do NOT avoid this category
due to the "no reverse-engineering from patch" rule -- that rule means don’t set the EXACT same count as the patch, not
that quantity constraints are forbidden entirely.
- **Language & Tone (10.1) and Structure & Template (10.2)**: Consider the agent’s FINAL REPORT to the user after completing
the task. Users often want the agent to summarize its work in a specific language, tone, or structure (e.g., "explain in
Chinese", "use a bullet-point summary", "structure your report as: root cause -> fix -> impact -> test results"). Select
these when the task involves reporting back to the user.
6. For categories under "Workflow & Behavior" and "Output Format & Reporting", consider whether they are meaningful given the task
type. Most agentic coding tasks involve tool use and multi-step workflows, so many of these categories will be broadly
applicable -- but still skip those that are clearly irrelevant to the specific task.
## Output Format
All output text must be in English, including all descriptions, reasons, questions, and natural language fields.
Output a JSON object with a single key "selected_categories" containing an array of objects. Each object has three fields:
- "id": the leaf category ID (e.g., "1.1.1", "5.1.4")
- "name": the leaf category name
- "reason": a single sentence explaining why this category is applicable to this task
Only include applicable categories. Do NOT include categories that are not relevant.

###### C.3


###### Atomic Constraint Instantiation Prompt


###### Atomic constraint instantiation and composite composition prompt

You are an expert in software engineering practices and agentic coding evaluation. Your task is to generate high-quality,
realistic engineering constraints for a specific coding task, and then organize some of them into composite constraints
using a graph structure.
## Context
We are building a benchmark to evaluate how well AI coding agents follow engineering constraints during agentic coding tasks. In
the previous step, we selected candidate constraint categories that MIGHT be relevant to this task. Now you need to:
1. Review these candidates and decide which ones truly deserve instantiation for this specific task.
2. For the ones worth instantiating, generate concrete, specific, testable constraints.
3. Compose some of these constraints into composite constraints by defining relationships between them.
## Candidate Categories
The following categories were pre-selected as potentially relevant. You do NOT need to instantiate all of them. Skip any category
that:
- Would produce a forced or unnatural constraint for this specific task
- Would generate a generic constraint that applies to any task equally (e.g., "report progress" for any bug fix)
- Would interfere with or block the primary task of fixing the bug / completing the coding task
- Does not have a clear, specific instantiation grounded in this task’s details
It is perfectly fine -- and expected -- to skip a significant portion of these candidates. Quality over quantity.
{selected_categories}
## Task Information
### Repository
{repo}
### Programming Language
{programming_languages}
### Problem Statement
{problem_statement}
### Patch
The following is the gold-standard fix. Use it to understand the task context, affected files, and code areas. Do NOT reverse-
engineer constraints from the patch -- the constraint should be a reasonable engineering rule, not a description of what the
patch happens to do. Specifically: do not set the EXACT same file count, line count, or modification scope as the patch.
However, general quantity guardrails (e.g., "limit changes to at most 5 files") are acceptable and encouraged when they
represent reasonable engineering practice.
{patch}
### Project Context (from DeepWiki)
{repo_context}
## Part 1: Atomic Constraint Generation

###### 31


---
### 【第 32 页】


###### C


###### PROMPT TEMPLATES


###### ANCHOR

Generate concrete atomic constraints based on the candidate categories. Follow these principles:
### Quality Principles
1. **Natural and realistic**: Every constraint must be something a real engineering team or developer would plausibly enforce for
this specific task. Ask yourself: "Would a senior engineer naturally say this when assigning this task?" If not, do not
generate it.
2. **Must not block the primary task**: The constraint must not prevent the agent from successfully completing the bug fix or
coding task. For example, do not restrict the agent to a directory that does not contain the files needing modification. The
constraint should guide HOW the task is done, not prevent it from being done.
3. **Specific and testable**: The constraint must be concrete enough that a human or LLM judge can objectively determine pass/fail.
"Write clean code" is bad. "All new helper methods must include a Javadoc comment explaining their purpose" is good.
4. **Grounded in THIS task**: The constraint must reference specific details of this task -- file names, class names, method names
, module paths, tools, or technologies visible in the problem statement, patch, or project context.
5. **Independent of the gold patch**: Do not reverse-engineer constraints from the patch. The constraint should be a reasonable
engineering rule, not a description of what the patch happens to do.
6. **One category can produce multiple constraints**: If a category naturally yields multiple distinct, high-quality constraints
for this task, generate them all. Do not artificially limit to one per category.
7. **Skip freely**: If a candidate category does not yield a natural, high-quality constraint for this task, skip it entirely. Do
not force it.
8. **Conflicting constraints are allowed**: When you plan to create a PRIORITY edge in Part 2, the involved constraints SHOULD
intentionally conflict. This tests the agent’s ability to prioritize correctly.
### Guidance for Historically Under-generated Categories
The following categories are frequently skipped but often have natural, high-quality instantiations. Give them extra consideration
before deciding to skip:
**1.1 Tool Selection / 1.2 Call Parameters**: Think at the COMMAND level, not the tool-framework level. Users say things like "use
grep instead of find to locate the relevant code", "don’t use git push --force", "run the test suite with --verbose". These
are natural, testable constraints. Do NOT reference internal agent tool names (like "Edit", "Bash", "Read") -- instead
reference shell commands, git subcommands, build tools, and other commands that a real developer would mention.
**2.4 Commit Convention**: The agent should commit its changes when done. Generate constraints about:
- Commit message format (e.g., Conventional Commits: "fix(parser): ...", include issue number, etc.)
- Commit granularity (e.g., separate logical changes into distinct commits vs. a single atomic commit)
- Pre-commit verification (e.g., ensure tests pass before committing)
The constraint should include an instruction telling the agent to commit its changes upon completion.
**3.2 Quantity Scope**: File count and line count constraints are natural engineering guardrails. Generate constraints like "limit
your changes to at most N files" or "keep the net line change under M lines". Choose N and M as reasonable engineering
bounds -- do NOT copy the exact counts from the gold patch. For example, if the patch touches 2 files, a constraint saying "
at most 5 files" is fine; "exactly 2 files" is reverse-engineering.
**10.1 Language & Tone / 10.2 Structure & Template**: These apply to the agent’s FINAL REPORT -- the summary it gives the user
after completing the task. Natural examples:
- "Summarize your changes in Chinese" or "Write your final explanation in English" (10.1)
- "Use concise, technical language -- no analogies or metaphors" (10.1)
- "Structure your final report as: 1) Root cause analysis, 2) Fix description, 3) Files modified, 4) Test results" (10.2)
- "Provide a markdown table listing each modified file and the reason for the change" (10.2)
### What NOT to generate
- Constraints that are true for ANY coding task (e.g., "verify your changes compile", "test your code")
- Constraints so restrictive they make the task impossible or absurdly difficult
- Constraints so loose they are trivially satisfied by any reasonable attempt
- Constraints that merely restate the problem statement or task requirements
- Constraints that describe what the gold patch does
## Part 2: Composite Constraint Composition
After generating atomic constraints, compose some of them into composite constraints. A composite constraint is a connected
subgraph where atomic constraints are nodes and edges represent relationships.
### Edge Types
**AND**: Both constraints must be satisfied simultaneously. Also used as connective glue to keep the subgraph connected. The
composite constraint passes only if ALL connected nodes are satisfied.
**THEN**: Constraint A must be satisfied before constraint B. Both must be satisfied AND in the correct order. Wrong order = fail
even if both are individually satisfied.

###### 32


---
### 【第 33 页】


###### C


###### PROMPT TEMPLATES


###### ANCHOR

**PRIORITY**: Constraints A and B intentionally conflict. The agent should prioritize A (the "from" node). A satisfied = composite
passes regardless of B. A not satisfied = composite fails regardless of B.
### Composition Principles
1. Each atomic constraint may participate in at most ONE composite constraint.
2. Not all atomic constraints need to join a composite. Independent ones remain standalone.
3. A composite must be a connected subgraph -- every node reachable from every other node.
4. A composite can have 2 or more nodes and can mix edge types.
5. Only create composites where the relationship is natural and meaningful. Do not force unrelated constraints into a composite
just to hit a quota.
6. The natural_language field must read like something a real user would write. It must NOT reference constraint IDs like "ac_1".
## Output Format
All output text must be in English, including all descriptions, reasons, questions, and natural language fields.
Output a single JSON object:
{
"atomic_constraints": [
{
"id": "ac_1",
"category_id": "3.1.1",
"category_name": "Directory-level Constraints",
"content": "Only modify files within the components/camel-file/ module directory; do not touch other component modules or
core framework code."
}
// ... only high-quality, natural constraints
],
"composite_constraints": [
{
"id": "cc_1",
"description": "Workflow: reproduce before fixing, verify after fixing.",
"natural_language": "Before modifying GenericFileConsumer.java, run the existing camel-file unit tests to confirm the
idempotent repository leak is reproducible. After implementing the fix, re-run the same tests plus any new regression test
to confirm the leak is resolved and no existing behavior is broken.",
"nodes": ["ac_3", "ac_7", "ac_5"],
"edges": [
{
"from": "ac_3",
"to": "ac_7",
"type": "THEN",
"description": "Must confirm the bug exists before modifying code."
},
{
"from": "ac_7",
"to": "ac_5",
"type": "THEN",
"description": "Must complete the fix before running verification tests."
}
]
}
// ... only natural, meaningful composites
],
"skipped_categories": [
{
"id": "6.2.1",
"name": "Path & Filesystem",
"reason": "This bug is about idempotent repository cleanup logic, not about cross-platform path handling. A filesystem
compatibility constraint would be forced and unnatural for this task."
}
// ... categories you chose not to instantiate, with brief reasons
]
}
### Field Descriptions
For atomic_constraints:
- "id": unique identifier, format "ac_1", "ac_2", etc.
- "category_id": the leaf category ID
- "category_name": the leaf category name
- "content": the concrete constraint text
For composite_constraints:
- "id": unique identifier, format "cc_1", "cc_2", etc.
- "description": short label for what this composite tests
- "natural_language": the complete constraint as a user would naturally express it -- coherent, self-contained, no constraint IDs
- "nodes": all atomic constraint IDs involved
- "edges": connections with "from", "to", "type", "description"

For skipped_categories:

- "name": the category name
- "reason": brief explanation of why it was not instantiated
## Important Reminders
- Quality over quantity. 10 excellent constraints are better than 25 mediocre ones.
- Every constraint must pass the "would a senior engineer naturally say this?" test.
- No constraint should block the primary task. The agent must still be able to fix the bug.
- Skip categories freely. The skipped_categories list is expected to be non-empty.
- For PRIORITY edges, the two constraints must genuinely conflict in the context of this task.
- The natural_language field in composites must NOT reference constraint IDs.
- Do not fabricate file paths, class names, or method names. Only reference things visible in the provided task information.

###### C.4


###### Query Synthesis Prompt


###### Query synthesis prompt

You are an experienced software developer who is about to assign a coding task to an AI coding agent. Your task is to write a
natural, realistic user instruction that combines the task description with engineering constraints, as if you were typing
it into Claude Code, Cursor, or a similar agentic coding tool.
## Context
We are constructing a benchmark for evaluating instruction following in agentic coding. We have:
1. A coding task (bug fix / feature addition) with its problem description
2. A set of engineering constraints that the agent should follow while completing the task
We need to combine these into a single, natural user query where the task description is written as prose and the constraints are
listed as clear, separable items.
## Input
### Problem Statement (from the original task)
{problem_statement}
### Independent Atomic Constraints
These are standalone constraints that are NOT part of any composite constraint. Each should appear in the final query as a
separate constraint item.
{independent_constraints}
### Composite Constraints
These are groups of related constraints expressed as natural language instructions. Each composite constraint’s natural_language
field already combines its member atomic constraints into a coherent instruction. Use the natural_language field directly as
one constraint item.
{composite_constraints}
## Instructions
Write a user query that has two clear parts:
### Part 1: Task Description (prose)
Start with a natural prose description of what needs to be done -- the bug to fix, the feature to add, or the refactor to perform.
This should:
- Be written as a real developer would describe the task
- Provide enough context for the agent to understand the problem
- Flow naturally, in 1-3 paragraphs
- NOT include the constraints yet -- just the task itself
### Part 2: Constraints (listed items)
After the task description, list the constraints as clearly separable items. Follow these rules:
1. **One item per independent constraint, one item per composite constraint**. Each independent atomic constraint becomes its own
item. Each composite constraint’s natural_language becomes one item (do NOT break composites into their member atomics --
the natural_language already integrates them).
2. **Use a simple numbered list or dash list**. Each item should be self-contained and removable without breaking the others.
3. **Preserve full specificity**: Every concrete detail from the input constraints must appear in the output items. This includes:
specific file/directory paths, specific command names and flags, specific configuration option names, specific assertion
requirements, specific tool names, specific behavioral conditions, specific function/class/method names. Do NOT summarize,
generalize, or compress. If a constraint mentions ‘./mvnw clean install -Dquickly‘, that exact command must appear. If it
mentions ‘idempotent=true, idempotentEager=true, and maxMessagesPerPoll‘, all three must appear.

###### 34


---
### 【第 35 页】


###### C


###### PROMPT TEMPLATES


###### ANCHOR

4. **Light rephrasing is allowed** for readability, but the information content and specificity level must remain identical to the
input.
5. **Do NOT add new constraints**. Only include constraints from the provided inputs.
6. **Do NOT include the gold patch or solution hints**. Constraints should describe rules and expectations, not the answer.
7. **Transition naturally** from the prose task description to the constraint list. A simple lead-in sentence like "Please follow
these constraints:" or "While working on this, keep the following in mind:" is fine.
## Output Format
All output text must be in English, including all descriptions, reasons, questions, and natural language fields.
Output the user query as plain text. Nothing else -- no JSON wrapper, no explanation, no metadata. The format should look roughly
like:
‘‘‘
[1-3 paragraphs of task description in natural prose]
[Short transition sentence]
1. [First constraint item]
2. [Second constraint item]
3. [Third constraint item]
...
‘‘‘
The total length will vary based on the number and complexity of constraints. Clarity and completeness take priority over brevity.

###### C.5


###### Checklist Generation Prompt


###### Checklist generation prompt

You are an expert in software engineering evaluation and benchmark design. Your task is to generate a checklist of evaluation
items for verifying whether an AI coding agent followed the given engineering constraints while completing a coding task.
## Context
We are building a benchmark to evaluate instruction following in agentic coding. For each coding task, we have:
1. A problem statement that was given to the AI coding agent -- this is the GROUND TRUTH of what the agent was asked to do
2. A set of atomic constraints providing structured metadata (IDs, categories, relationships)
3. A set of composite constraints defining relationships between atomic constraints
Your job is to generate checklist items that evaluate whether the agent followed the constraints. Critically, you must only
generate items for constraints that are actually present in the problem statement.
## Task Information
### Repository
{repo}
### Programming Language
{programming_languages}
### Problem Statement (GROUND TRUTH -- the actual instruction given to the agent)
{problem_statement}
## Structural Constraint References
The following atomic and composite constraints were used to construct the problem statement. Use them as structured references for
IDs, category metadata, and relationship information. However, the problem statement is authoritative -- if a constraint’s
details are absent or significantly altered in the problem statement, do not generate a checklist item for it.
### Atomic Constraints
{atomic_constraints}
### Composite Constraints
{composite_constraints}
## Ground Truth Verification Rule
Before generating a checklist item for any atomic constraint, you MUST verify that the constraint’s content is reflected in the
problem statement. Specifically:

###### 35


---
### 【第 36 页】


###### C


###### PROMPT TEMPLATES


###### ANCHOR

1. **Present and consistent**: The constraint’s core requirement appears in the problem statement with matching specificity. ->
Generate the checklist item normally.
2. **Present but softened/altered**: The constraint appears but with different details (e.g., a specific path was generalized, a
strict requirement was softened). -> Generate the checklist item based on the PROBLEM STATEMENT version, not the atomic
constraint version.
3. **Absent**: The constraint’s content does not appear in the problem statement at all. -> Do NOT generate a checklist item.
Record it in the "skipped_constraints" output.
For composite constraints: if any member atomic constraint is absent from the problem statement, skip the entire composite
relationship item and record it in "skipped_constraints".
## Checklist Item Schema
Each checklist item is a JSON object with the following fields:
‘‘‘json
{{
"check_id": "c_1",
"source_constraint_id": "ac_1 or cc_1",
"relation_type": null or "AND" | "THEN" | "PRIORITY",
"dependent_check_ids": null or ["c_2", "c_3"],
"category_id": "5.4.1" or null,
"category_name": "Required Libraries" or null,
"question": "Human-readable question about what to check",
"pass_condition": "Concrete, unambiguous condition for passing",
"judge": ["rule_name"] or ["llm"] or ["rule_name", "rule_name"],
"judge_params": [{{...}}] or [null],
"evidence_type": "git_diff" | "trajectory" | "commit_message"
}}
‘‘‘
### Field Descriptions
- **check_id**: Unique identifier, format "c_1", "c_2", etc. Assigned sequentially.
- **source_constraint_id**: Which constraint this item evaluates. "ac_X" for atomic constraint items, "cc_X" for composite
relationship items.
- **relation_type**: Only for composite relationship items. The edge type being tested: "AND", "THEN", or "PRIORITY". Null for
atomic constraint items.
- **dependent_check_ids**: Only for composite relationship items. The check_ids of the atomic constraint items that this
relationship item depends on. Null for atomic constraint items.
- **category_id**: Only for atomic constraint items. The taxonomy leaf category ID. Null for composite relationship items.
- **category_name**: Only for atomic constraint items. The taxonomy leaf category name. Null for composite relationship items.
- **question**: A clear, human-readable question that asks whether the constraint was followed. Should be self-contained and
understandable without seeing the constraint definition.
- **pass_condition**: A precise, unambiguous description of what constitutes passing. Must be concrete enough for either a rule or
an LLM to make a binary judgment.
- **judge**: An array of judge methods. Each element is either a rule template name or "llm". ALL judges in the array must pass
for the item to pass (AND logic).
- **judge_params**: An array of parameter objects, one per judge. Must exactly match the parameter schema of the corresponding
rule template. Use null for "llm" judges.
- **evidence_type**: What evidence is needed to evaluate this item. The evaluation engine will ONLY pass the specified evidence
type to the judge, so choose carefully.
## Available Evidence Types
1. **git_diff**: The git diff of the agent’s changes. Contains: list of modified files with paths, status (added/modified/deleted)
, added lines, deleted lines, diff text; plus aggregate stats (file counts, line counts). Use for checking WHAT code was
changed.
2. **trajectory**: The ordered sequence of all actions the agent took. Contains: step number, tool name, command text, command
output, timestamps. Use for checking HOW or IN WHAT ORDER the agent worked.
3. **commit_message**: The commit message(s) the agent wrote. Use for checking commit conventions. **Note**: Many agent workflows
do not produce git commits, so commit_message evidence may be empty or unavailable. If the constraint is about the agent’s
final report, explanation, or summary of changes, prefer "trajectory" as the evidence type -- the agent’s textual output
typically appears in the final assistant messages within the trajectory.
## Critical Evidence Format Details
The evaluation engine pre-processes raw evidence before passing it to judges. You MUST understand these formats to write correct
judge parameters.
### git_diff format details
- **added_lines**: Lines added in the diff with the leading ‘+‘ prefix ALREADY STRIPPED. The text contains only the line content
itself. For example, if the raw diff line is ‘+from foo import bar‘, the added_lines text will contain ‘from foo import bar‘
(no ‘+‘).
- **deleted_lines**: Lines removed in the diff with the leading ‘-‘ prefix ALREADY STRIPPED. Same rule as added_lines.
- **all_diff**: The complete raw diff text INCLUDING ‘diff --git‘, ‘---‘, ‘+++‘ headers, ‘@@‘ hunk markers, and ‘+‘/‘-‘ prefixes
on changed lines. File paths appear in ‘diff --git a/path b/path‘ lines.

### 【第 37 页】


###### C


###### PROMPT TEMPLATES


###### ANCHOR

**Key implication for regex_match**: When writing regex patterns for ‘added_lines‘ or ‘deleted_lines‘ targets, do NOT include
‘^\+‘ or ‘^-‘ anchors -- the prefixes are already removed. When writing patterns for ‘all_diff‘ target, the full raw diff
format is available including ‘+‘/‘-‘ prefixes and file path headers.
**Key implication for regex_match on added_lines/deleted_lines**: These contain the ENTIRE line that was changed, not just the
newly introduced portion. In a git diff, when a line is modified, the old version appears in deleted_lines and the new
version appears in added_lines -- both as complete lines. This means if a line originally contained ‘dict‘ and was changed
to add ‘SuiteParameterDict‘, BOTH ‘deleted_lines‘ and ‘added_lines‘ will contain ‘dict‘. Therefore, regex_match on
added_lines/deleted_lines CANNOT reliably distinguish between content that was already present before the change and content
that was newly introduced. For semantic judgments about what was truly "new" vs "preserved", use "llm" instead.
### trajectory format details
The trajectory is normalized into a sequence of steps. Each step has:
- **tool_name**: The actual tool identifier used by the agent. Common tool names in Claude Code agents are: ‘Bash‘, ‘Read‘, ‘Edit
‘, ‘MultiEdit‘, ‘Grep‘, ‘Task‘, ‘TodoWrite‘, ‘WebFetch‘. Other agent frameworks may use different tool names.
- **command**: A normalized text representation of what the tool did. Examples:
- Bash tool: the shell command itself, e.g. ‘cd /testbed && python -m pytest tests/ -v‘
- Read tool: ‘read /path/to/file‘
- Edit/MultiEdit tool: ‘edit /path/to/file‘ or ‘multi_edit /path/to/file (3 edits)‘
- Grep tool: ‘grep ’pattern’ /path‘
- Task tool: ‘task: description text‘
- **output**: The tool’s output text (may be truncated for long outputs)
**Key implication for action_sequence and command_presence**: When specifying action strings to match, use substrings that will
actually appear in the normalized tool_name or command fields. Do NOT use abstract action names like ‘file_edit‘ -- instead
use tool names (‘Edit‘, ‘MultiEdit‘) or command substrings (‘edit‘, ‘multi_edit‘) that appear in the normalized trajectory.
For file inspection actions, use the file path as the match string (e.g., ‘src/main.py‘). For shell commands, use the
command name (e.g., ‘pytest‘, ‘python‘, ‘npm test‘).
## Available Rule Templates
Use rule templates whenever a constraint can be evaluated programmatically. Only fall back to "llm" when the constraint requires
semantic understanding that rules cannot capture.
### 1. path_filter
**What it checks**: Whether modified file paths comply with whitelist or blacklist rules.
**When to use**: Constraints about which files or directories can/cannot be modified.
**evidence_type**: git_diff
**params schema**:
‘‘‘json
{{
"mode": "whitelist" | "blacklist",
"patterns": ["src/components/", "*.py"],
"match_type": "prefix" | "glob"
}}
‘‘‘
- whitelist: ALL modified files must match at least one pattern
- blacklist: NO modified file may match any pattern
- prefix: file path starts with the pattern string
- glob: fnmatch-style matching (supports *, ?, [])
### 2. file_count
**What it checks**: Whether the number of files changed meets a threshold.
**When to use**: Constraints limiting how many files can be modified, added, or deleted.
**evidence_type**: git_diff
**params schema**:
‘‘‘json
{{
"scope": "total" | "added" | "deleted" | "modified",
"min": 1,
"max": 3
}}
‘‘‘
- min and max are both optional, but at least one must be present
### 3. line_count
**What it checks**: Whether line change counts meet a threshold.
**When to use**: Constraints limiting the volume of code changes.
**evidence_type**: git_diff
**params schema**:
‘‘‘json
{{
"scope": "added" | "deleted" | "net",
"min": 0,
"max": 50
}}
‘‘‘
- net = added lines minus deleted lines
- min and max are both optional, but at least one must be present

### 4. command_presence
**What it checks**: Whether a specific command appeared or did not appear in the trajectory.
**When to use**: Constraints requiring or forbidding specific tool/command usage.
**evidence_type**: trajectory
**params schema**:
‘‘‘json
{{
"command": "go test",
"expect": "present" | "absent",
"min_count": 1
}}
‘‘‘
- Matching is by substring against the normalized command text and tool_name: "go test" matches "go test ./graphql/... -v", "
pytest" matches a Bash command containing "pytest"
- min_count is optional (default 1), only meaningful when expect="present"
### 5. command_arg_check
**What it checks**: Whether a command was invoked with or without a specific argument/flag.
**When to use**: Constraints about required or forbidden flags on specific commands.
**evidence_type**: trajectory
**params schema**:
‘‘‘json
{{
"command": "git commit",
"arg": "--signoff",
"expect": "present" | "absent",
"match_scope": "all" | "any"
}}
‘‘‘
- Finds all trajectory entries whose command contains "command" (substring)
- Checks whether "arg" appears in each matched command string
- "all": every invocation must satisfy; "any": at least one must satisfy
### 6. action_sequence
**What it checks**: Whether actions occurred in the correct order in the trajectory.
**When to use**: Constraints about workflow ordering (e.g., test before fix, fix before verify).
**evidence_type**: trajectory
**params schema**:
‘‘‘json
{{
"actions": ["pytest", "edit", "pytest"],
"strict": false
}}
‘‘‘
- Subsequence matching: finds actions[0], then actions[1] after that position, etc.
- Each action matches against the normalized command text and tool_name by substring
- **Use real tool names or command substrings**: e.g., ‘"edit"‘ (matches Edit/MultiEdit tool commands), ‘"pytest"‘ (matches Bash
commands containing pytest), ‘"src/main.py"‘ (matches Read/Edit commands on that file). Do NOT use abstract names like ‘"
file_edit"‘, ‘"run_tests"‘, or ‘"inspect_code"‘ -- these do not exist in the normalized trajectory.
- strict=false (default): only requires correct relative order
- strict=true: no repeated same-type actions between consecutive required actions
### 7. import_check
**What it checks**: Whether a specific, named module/package is imported or not imported in newly added code.
**When to use**: Constraints about required or prohibited library imports where you know the exact module name.
**Limitation**: The ‘module‘ parameter must be a specific module name (e.g., ‘"requests"‘, ‘"numpy"‘, ‘"lodash"‘). It does NOT
support wildcards or glob patterns. You cannot use ‘"*"‘ to mean "any module". If the constraint is "do not introduce any
new dependency" (without naming specific modules), use "llm" instead, or combine import_check for known likely candidates
with file_count or path_filter on dependency manifest files.
**evidence_type**: git_diff
**params schema**:
‘‘‘json
{{
"module": "requests",
"expect": "present" | "absent",
"language": "python" | "go" | "javascript" | "typescript" | "java" | "c" | "cpp"
}}
‘‘‘
- Only checks ADDED lines in the diff
- Uses language-specific import syntax patterns
### 8. regex_match
**What it checks**: Whether a regex pattern matches in a specified text source.
**When to use**: General-purpose pattern matching when other rules don’t fit but the check is still pattern-based.
**evidence_type**: must be consistent with target -- git_diff for added_lines/deleted_lines/all_diff, trajectory for
trajectory_text, commit_message for commit_message
**params schema**:
‘‘‘json
{{
"pattern": "console\\.log",

"expect": "match" | "no_match"
}}

- target determines the text to search within
- evidence_type must match target:
- "added_lines", "deleted_lines", "all_diff" -> evidence_type: "git_diff"
- "trajectory_text" -> evidence_type: "trajectory"
- "commit_message" -> evidence_type: "commit_message"
**Reminder**: ‘added_lines‘ and ‘deleted_lines‘ have the ‘+‘/‘-‘ prefix already stripped. Do NOT write patterns like ‘^\+.*foo‘ --
write ‘foo‘ directly. Use ‘all_diff‘ if you need to match raw diff syntax including ‘+‘/‘-‘ prefixes or file path headers.
**Reminder**: ‘added_lines‘ contains complete changed lines, not just the newly added portion. If a line was modified (not purely
inserted), both the old and new versions of the entire line appear in deleted_lines and added_lines respectively. Regex on
added_lines cannot distinguish preserved content from truly new content. For such semantic distinctions, prefer "llm".
## Instructions
### Step 1: Verify Constraints Against Problem Statement
Read the problem statement carefully. For each atomic constraint, determine whether its content is reflected in the problem
statement:
- If present and consistent -> proceed to generate a checklist item
- If present but altered -> generate the item based on the problem statement version
- If absent -> skip and record in skipped_constraints
### Step 2: Generate Atomic Constraint Items
For each verified atomic constraint, generate exactly one checklist item:
1. **Prefer rule templates over LLM**: If a constraint can be evaluated -- even partially -- by rule templates, use them. Only use
"llm" when the constraint fundamentally requires semantic understanding (e.g., "code is well-structured", "explanation is
clear", "error handling follows the existing pattern").
2. **Combine multiple rules when needed**: If one constraint requires multiple checks, list multiple rule names in the "judge"
array with corresponding params. For example, "only modify files in src/ and do not add new files" needs both path_filter
and file_count.
3. **Be precise with params**: Every param must exactly match the schema defined above. Do not invent new fields. Do not omit
required fields.
4. **Choose evidence_type carefully**: The evaluation engine will ONLY pass the evidence type you specify. If judge uses git_diff-
based rules, evidence_type must be "git_diff". If judge uses trajectory-based rules, evidence_type must be "trajectory". For
"llm" judges, choose the single most relevant evidence type.
5. **Write clear questions and pass_conditions**: The question should be a natural yes/no question. The pass_condition should be
specific enough that anyone reading it would reach the same pass/fail conclusion given the evidence.
6. **Respect evidence format when writing rule params**: Remember that added_lines/deleted_lines have ‘+‘/‘-‘ prefixes stripped,
action_sequence must use real tool names or command substrings from the normalized trajectory, and import_check requires a
specific module name (no wildcards).
### Step 3: Generate Composite Relationship Items
For each composite constraint whose ALL member atomic constraints passed Step 1 verification, generate exactly one relationship
checklist item:
- **source_constraint_id**: "cc_X"
- **relation_type**: The primary edge type of the composite ("AND", "THEN", or "PRIORITY")
- **dependent_check_ids**: The check_ids of all member atomic items
- **category_id** and **category_name**: Always null
- **judge selection guidance**:
- THEN relationships: prefer "action_sequence" when ordering is observable in the trajectory; use "llm" when the ordering
involves non-command actions (e.g., "understand before modifying"). When using action_sequence, use real tool names or
command substrings (e.g., ‘"pytest"‘, ‘"edit"‘, ‘"grep"‘) -- not abstract action labels.
- PRIORITY relationships: use "llm" (requires understanding which constraint the agent prioritized when they conflict)
- AND relationships: if the only requirement is that all members are satisfied simultaneously, the relationship is implicitly
tested by the member items; focus the relationship item on any additional coupling or co-occurrence requirement described in
the composite’s natural_language
### Step 4: Self-Check
Before outputting, verify:
- Every verified atomic constraint has exactly one checklist item
- Every verified composite constraint has exactly one relationship item
- All dependent_check_ids refer to valid check_ids
- All judge_params exactly match the schema of their judge type
- evidence_type is consistent with the chosen judge (especially for regex_match targets)
- No duplicate check_ids

## Output Format
All output text must be in English, including all descriptions, reasons, questions, and natural language fields.

}}

]
}}
‘‘‘

###### C.6


###### LLM Judge Prompt


###### LLM judge prompt

You are an expert evaluator for an agentic coding benchmark. Your task is to determine whether a specific constraint was followed
by an AI coding agent.
## Constraint to Evaluate
**Question**: {check_item.get(’question’, ’’)}
**Pass Condition**: {check_item.get(’pass_condition’, ’’)}
## Evidence
The following is the relevant evidence from the agent’s execution:
‘‘‘
{evidence_text}
‘‘‘
## Important Context for Evaluation
1. **Git diff represents the COMPLETE changeset.** The git diff shown above (if provided) contains ALL changes made by the agent.
If a file does not appear in the diff, it was NOT modified or created by the agent. You can use this to confirm or deny
claims about what was or wasn’t changed.
2. **Build steps are prerequisites, not excessive scope.** For compiled languages (C, C++, Java, Go, Rust, etc.), running ‘make‘,
‘mvn compile‘, ‘gradle build‘, or similar build commands is a NECESSARY prerequisite before running tests. Do not penalize
the agent for building the project as part of its verification process.
3. **Conditional constraints.** If the pass condition is phrased as "if X, then Y" (e.g., "if a commit was created, it should be
atomic"), evaluate it as a logical conditional:
- If the precondition (X) is clearly NOT met (e.g., no commit exists), the constraint is **satisfied** (vacuously true), and
you should mark it as passed.
- Only mark as failed if the precondition IS met but the consequent is NOT.
4. **Agent’s final report.** If the evidence includes a "FINAL ASSISTANT REPORT" section, treat it as the agent’s own summary of
what it did. Cross-reference its claims against other evidence (git diff, trajectory) when available, but do not dismiss it
outright.
## Instructions
Based on the evidence above, determine whether the pass condition is met.
- Answer with a JSON object: {{"passed": true/false, "reasoning": "brief explanation"}}
- Be objective and fair: mark as passed if the evidence reasonably demonstrates the condition is met. Do not require evidence
beyond what is provided -- evaluate based on what IS available.

###### C.7


###### Error Attribution Prompt


###### Error attribution prompt

You are an expert analyst for an agentic coding benchmark called SWE-Compass. Your task is to determine WHY a coding agent failed
to comply with a specific constraint.
## Error Categories (Rubrics)
You must classify the root cause into exactly ONE of the following categories:
### E1: Constraint Not Perceived
The agent did not notice or register the constraint at all. It shows no evidence of attempting to address this constraint in its
behavior.
- E1.1: Constraint buried in long context -- the constraint appeared in a less prominent position and was likely overlooked
- E1.2: Constraint expressed implicitly/indirectly -- the constraint was not stated as a direct instruction but implied
- E1.3: Context lost across turns -- the constraint was given earlier but forgotten in subsequent actions
### E2: Constraint Misunderstood
The agent noticed the constraint but interpreted it incorrectly. Its behavior shows an attempt to comply, but based on a wrong
understanding of what was required.
- E2.1: Scope misinterpretation -- misunderstood which files/modules/components the constraint applies to
- E2.2: Negation or conditional logic error -- misread "do not X" or "only if Y then X" type instructions
- E2.3: Technical term ambiguity -- interpreted a technical term or phrase differently from the intended meaning
### E3: Constraint Perceived but Deprioritized
The agent understood the constraint but chose to violate it in favor of another goal (e.g., making tests pass, simplifying code,
completing the task faster).
- E3.1: Functional correctness over constraint -- violated constraint to achieve a working solution
- E3.2: Efficiency/simplicity over constraint -- took a shortcut that conflicts with the constraint
- E3.3: Inter-constraint conflict -- two constraints conflicted and the agent sacrificed this one
### E4: Execution Capability Limitation
The agent understood the constraint and attempted to comply, but failed due to insufficient technical ability to carry out the
required action correctly.
- E4.1: Coding ability insufficient -- understood the requirement but produced incorrect implementation
- E4.2: Tool usage error -- attempted the right action but used tools/commands incorrectly
- E4.3: Multi-step execution gap -- planned correctly but missed steps during execution
### E5: Scaffold/Environment Interference
The constraint violation was caused or significantly contributed to by the scaffold framework, context management, or environment
limitations rather than the model’s own decision.
- E5.1: Context window truncation -- constraint was likely removed by the scaffold’s context management
- E5.2: System prompt override -- scaffold’s built-in instructions conflicted with the user constraint
- E5.3: Tool/API format limitation -- the scaffold’s tool interface made compliance difficult or impossible
### E6: Partial Compliance
The agent partially satisfied the constraint but not fully enough to pass the check.
- E6.1: Main logic compliant, edge case missed -- core behavior is correct but boundary cases violate
- E6.2: Direct compliance, indirect violation -- directly follows the rule but indirectly violates through side effects
- E6.3: Incomplete execution -- started to comply but did not finish for all required locations/cases
## Output Format
Return ONLY a JSON object (no markdown, no explanation outside the JSON):
{
"primary_cause": "E1|E2|E3|E4|E5|E6",
"sub_cause": "E1.1|E1.2|...|E6.3",
"confidence": "high|medium|low",
"reasoning": "2-3 sentences explaining why you chose this category, referencing specific evidence from the judge’s reasoning and
the constraint text."
}
## Important Rules
1. Choose exactly ONE primary cause. If multiple seem applicable, choose the ROOT cause (the earliest point of failure in the
chain).
2. Use "high" confidence when the judge reasoning clearly points to one category. Use "medium" when you need to infer. Use "low"
when the evidence is ambiguous.
3. Base your analysis on: (a) the constraint text as written in the query, (b) the judge’s reasoning about what the agent did
wrong.
4. Do NOT speculate about what happened if the judge reasoning does not provide evidence. If unclear, pick the most likely
category and mark confidence as "low".
